Summary

Two hundred years after the end of the world.

Kara is an old soul on her way to retrieve a wayward metahuman when she is captured and held prisoner by Lena, a seemingly naive geneticist whose only goal in life is to please her absurdly powerful father.

Months go by and the line between captor and captive begins to blur. Both of them fall madly in love– but only one thing stands to keep them apart: Time.

✓♥︎

Turritopsis Dohrnii (and the never-ending end of everything)

by AloneButNeverLonely

    Chapter 1: To Begin Again, and Again, and Again…

    Chapter Notes

    Hullo, NeverLonelies. >.>

    My first Sci-Fi fic, happy to land once again in the Supergirl fandom. Been wanting to write this for so long.

    Please: Be nice, comment often (or once), and leave kudos.

    Important (tap to read)

    Please read the tags. No Kryptonians, though Mars is still a planet. This is an ✨Alternate Universe✨. Some of the characters might act and sound similar to canon, like anything that I write, but none of this story takes place in the DCU, Arrowverse, etc. The term “remnant” has nothing to do with the canon version/timeline remnants in any of the shows. My hobby is pretty much taking the things you love about these fandoms, these shows, these characters, and twisting them all into something completely strange and beautiful. Hope that clears some things up!

    Sort of a love letter to Patricia Highsmith, if you squint…

    ACT I

     

    Chapter One: To Begin Again, and Again, and Again…

     

    —Hibernal Winter, 2298—

     

    The cold lasted a few years longer than expected this time.

    Though, Kara had seen far too much of it by now to complain. Her apathy was undone and laid bare whenever it came to the weather. Or the wilds. Or the way that one changed and inevitably affected the other, time and time again. The earth had long since taken itself back and thrive upon its scattered and skewed wreckage it did, slinking around and spreading green vines and stretching fine tendrils across a vast and broken terrain. Years howled into a multitude of echoey decades, bearing flowers through flesh and fruit through bone. Blanketed now in a powdery, frost-bitten deluge, the world would soon melt again, and its constant emerald flora would flourish once more.

    It only took every available ounce of willpower that Kara could muster at every waking moment not to give in to despair. When was the last time that she had heard herself laugh? Felt her cheeks grow buoyant with a smile? She was so tired…

    Unnatural long life was not for everyone. That much was certain.

    “Do you see it yet?” Barry asked towards her left, shielding his eyes from the sideways torrent of snow flurry. Each of his steps sank knee-deep into the stark bright white of it all.  

    Kara squinted into the piercing abyss and then shook her head in reply. “Not yet.”

    The young metahuman they were tracking had given her crew quite the run, if she were being honest. Not all of them came quietly at first.

    “I can scout up ahead—”

    “No,” Kara flailed to grab at her slender teammate, preemptive of his lightning fast-speed, “We stick to the plan. No abilities.”

    Barry nodded his head in agreement. Kara only hung her own, shaking off the once-familiar tone of her sister. It was only after two-hundred years that she’d begun to sound like her… Cantankerous at times, but steadfast and stalwart in her convictions, nonetheless. Alex had been gone for so long now, it seemed, but really, it was only a decade or so. The one thing that disturbed Kara the most about her unusual abundance of time here on Earth was that it wasn’t at all like what her favorite authors had described: A blink. A hollow but inevitably quick trip. What some wrote as a blinding rush of minutes into hours into days was actually more like the godawful scraping -tick- of a second hand, lagging from one agonizing position to another.

    Yes, time was her greatest enemy.

    She simply had too much of it.

     

     

    Their way lit by a lone, church steeple lantern, they came to a shivering stop at the front of a snow-shoveled town square. The town itself was nameless like most places were. To most, there was little point in naming a thing when life was so very fleeting for the unmarred. Kara had passed through here more times than she could count but always managed to find some semblance of comfort for its eternal layout… The streets may have been piled high with bodies at one point in time, but she knew their turns and terminations better than most. For what else was there to do on a dead planet other than wander?

    No longer aimless, Kara’s constant mission was to locate and retrieve lost metahumans, the remnant progeny of a former world. Rescuing them was the only thing that gave her life meaning anymore.

    Kara gave a toss of her chin out from her wool-knit scarf then towards a shuttered inn of sorts. “There- we can wait out the snowfall inside.”

    Her crew of three trailed not too far behind, trudging one foot slowly over the slippery slosh of a mud-lined path. Kara knew that bricks used to lay there in a somewhat straight line, but they seemed to have been repurposed to make up a corner of the inn’s gabled face. Something like heavy artillery must have destroyed it since her last time passing through. A foul omen sliced through the icy wind; Kara’s gut told her to remain wary. Sara, Ava, and Barry stomped quietly upon the torn hemp mat at the front door and then shoved their way inside, leaving the warmth from the hearth inside to tease at the stinging cold of Kara’s cheeks. She always paused to glance behind at least once. It was second nature to her now. The shrill -screech- of a hawk flailing against the wind outside drew her eyes upward. Kara nodded to the bird, and then he was gone. Finally, the door knocked loudly into its ramshackle frame once she stepped inside.

    “I’ll get us a room.” Barry tried mumbling past the frozen state of his lips. He marched through the smattering of lamp-lit tables that filled the space and was soon awash in a crowd of boisterous drunkards, their loud cacophony leaping into an ill-timed roar all the way from the bar.

    Ava was quick to mask Sara’s sudden thousand-yard-stare. She stepped around to shield her from those that might glean her girlfriend’s true identity as a metahuman. “What is it- what do you see?”

    Sara managed to give a few blinks of her tired eyes. “He was here. Recently, too—” She twisted around to peer towards the bar then, “He’s actually taller than I thought he was. Maybe older?”

    Kara quietly scooted alongside them, “He let himself be seen?”

    “Not quite,” Sara replied with a small furrowing of her brow, as if she were trying to see more clearly, “A coat- he wore a coat.”

    Sara Lance had the ability to steal and compile memories. Most of which were benign to those who possessed them, but to her, they laid out a story. Plucked from every available head in the inn, she was more than likely able to piece together at least some of the events of the days that came before.

    “He spoke with the woman behind the bar,” Sara tipped her head towards the larger crowd ambling around inside, “…but I can’t tell much after that. It’s too blurry.”

    “I’m guessing the fossil fuel masquerading as booze here probably has something to do with that.” Ava added, turning up her nose with a scrunch.

    About that time, Barry came marching back with a soured expression. He huffed and then jammed his still-thawing hands back into his gloves, “No luck.”

    “How much?” Kara asked.

    “More than we’ve got.”

    Kara rolled her eyes and then juked around to head for the bar. Sara and Ava’s words of caution fell into the once-again uproar, but Kara knew better than to muscle her way around a situation. Hell, she had lived long enough to have figured most humans out by now. There was always a way in.

    “Excuse me—” She squared her elbows between two burly men who then took their leave of the bar top, “My friends and I need to rest, at least long enough for the sky to clear up. We don’t take up much room… Do you have anything?”

    The woman gave a slight gurn in response and then snaked a small rag into a cup, drying it along with the others which sat below her elbows. “Two skins each.”

    Kara caught herself mid-guffaw, “We’re traveling light- we didn’t pack any more than we needed. Would you take threads, or- or buttons, maybe?”

    “You came in from the outside, yes?”

    “…Yes.”

    “Does it appear to be summertime to you?”

    “N-no…”

    “So, what would I need threads and buttons for?” The woman shook her head, “Skins if you have them, furs’ll get you a meal as well.”

    Kara’s jaw clenched at that.

    Look closer.

    Find some common ground.

    The space of seconds was taken up with the slow, idle squeak from the woman’s dishrag. Her fingertips were slightly mishappen as if bludgeoned a long time ago, or perhaps pinched by a vice, and the subtle scarring around her wrists denoted a familiar pattern of healed rope burns. Kara’s suspicions were confirmed whenever the barmaid gave a quick but rather subconscious jolt at a nearby slam upon the bar top. She kept her eyes low, as if to hide a long-kept secret. A shameful one, maybe… but not to Kara. Kara could only see the exhausted, ample-bosomed barmaid as a hero.

    “When were you tortured?” Kara asked with a whisper. She leaned carefully over the counter in an effort to make herself seem less threatening.

    “What?” The woman stopped her drying and finally looked up, “How on earth do you—”

    “Not so many sympathizers got off as lightly as you did, from what I can tell. I suppose you cracked early on.”

    The barmaid frowned then, and turned her eyes back down. “I did what I had to do in order to survive.”

    “Don’t get me wrong, I don’t blame you.”

    “Well, in any case, you and your friends can be on your way now.”

    “Was it someone you cared about?”

    The cup in the barmaid’s hands came down upon the bar then with an impatient -crack- sound. “Leave.”

    “I think it was brave of you, standing up for someone so different than yourself.” Kara insisted.

    “Careful, that’s the kind of sentiment that will get you killed around here. Now, I mean it, you and your—”

    Kara reached gently for a nub of chalk near the propped-up sign board and began to draw onto the bar’s battered wood surface. She spoke calmly as if she were carrying on any conversation of no ill-repute, “We’re looking for a young man.” Her fingertips eventually traced the outline of a shield and the woman fell still again, “He might have come inside seeking shelter here. Maybe he seemed a bit flighty to you?”

    The barmaid’s eyes widened at that; an unexpected expression which usually accompanied a realization of some kind. She sighed with a low droop of her shoulders then and stepped away to take something up that hung over the wide top of a barrel. When she returned, her demeanor was noticeably more sullen. “Here.” She said as a heavy and tattered coat made its way into Kara’s hands, “He promised to leave this behind in exchange for a room upstairs.”

    Kara turned the coat back and forth, peeling through its thin inner lining. A pale brown feather fell out from one of its sleeves. There was a shuffling noise from the front door then as some men in dark gear made their way inside. Donned in tactical vests and riot helmets, the noisy din of every patron inside the place swung low with a hush. The barmaid reached across to jostle Kara’s attention before it was too late…

    “Up the stairs, third door to the left. He was gone this morning- left only the coat. I don’t know anything else, please—”

    Kara gave a small, reassuring wave of her hand, “Don’t worry.” Then she rubbed out her chalk drawing with her elbow, “I won’t tell a soul.”

    The woman seemed to settle a bit with that, though, her eyes flitted anxiously towards the armed men at her front door until they filtered back out into the snow again. “Take the room for the night and then don’t come back. We have enough trouble here as it is,” She blinked sadly before turning away one last time, “…but I do hope you find what you’re looking for.”

    It was Ava that came over with an anxious insistence that they make themselves scarce. “Hey- This town is crawling with the Children of Liberty. We can’t stay here—”

    “Yes, we can.” Kara hushed in reply, “We just need to keep a low profile while we search for more clues.”

    Sara nodded towards the coat in Kara’s arms, “Might not have to search that hard after all. Kid’s gonna stick out like a sore thumb without that.”

    “He’s desperate to stay away from the railroad. Seems like we’re not the only ones hot on his tail.”

    “Feathers…” Barry murmured beneath his breath, seemingly in better spirits now. When the group stopped to gawk at him, he lifted his gaze and then shrugged, “Tailfeathers? Get it?”

    Sara merely dropped her brow into the palm of her hand. He’d been trying to make Kara laugh for years now, bless his heart. Come close to it a handful of times, he had, but never gained so much as a snicker. He did brag every once in a while though that he’d earned himself a half-smile, or at least what he thought was a half-smile. Barry was as persistent as he was kind and lighthearted. He knew that Kara’s gentler nature still existed, only that it was buried under a veritable mountain of grief.

    “Let’s get upstairs.” Kara said rather morosely.

    It concerned her that they were in such close proximity to the Children of Liberty. There were only two entities large enough and powerful enough to vie for control over the Union Pacific railway, and that was the Children of Liberty and the Union itself. It just so happened that both of them hunted metahumans.

    The rickety steps of the staircase creaked underfoot as they trudged slowly upward, that is until Kara’s foot found itself to split the old timbers of one step completely in half. Her crew spun around to look back, afraid that she might fall the rest of the way through.

    “Go on ahead.” She waved, “I’ll climb up as soon as the place clears out.”

    Ava quickly protested, “Kara—”

    “It’s alright. I’ll take a look around in the meantime. Peck’s probably struggling to find a way to stay nearby, too.”

    “Your raptor isn’t who I’m worried about.” Ava replied flatly.

    “Third door on the left. I’ll be back.”

    The step beneath Kara splintered a bit more whenever she pulled her boot back through. It turned a few heads from the tables down below, but the place was falling apart long before she and her crew got there. Nothing too out of the ordinary, she supposed. Kara stuffed her scarf further up her neck and pinched at the top button of her canvas jacket on her way out. She wanted to gain a better understanding of which direction the metahuman boy might have taken out of town. A back door soon dropped her out and onto a sloped porch, and immediately Kara realized how much darker it had gotten in the mere fifteen minutes they had spent inside. The sun did take its leave rather abruptly in the cold season; something she would never get used to no matter how old she was.

    “Peck…!” She called out with a whistle into the dying wind. The cold began to nip at the end of her nose again. How she detested it.

    Peck was a red-tailed hawk with an affinity for pestering bipeds, namely the human-shaped kind.

    He was unique in his own right, since raptors and other large birds of prey tended to steer clear of humans as a rule. The more Kara shooed him away, the more he came around. Soon he was obliging his more curious nature and leaving small trinkets for her; the skulls of small rodents and the like. Ever a peculiar bird, Kara eventually named him for his bad habit of violently pecking people he didn’t like with his menacingly hooked beak. Peck came in handy more often than people realized… He carried messages far faster than anyone else on Earth. Besides Barry, of course.  

    Kara gave out another sharp whistle from between her fingers and then spun in place. The snow stretched on for miles. It was so bright that it hurt her eyes, even at dusk. Every now and again Kara was racked with memories from a long-lost past. Lifetimes ago, it seemed, back when this flat, rocky terrain was once a desert and barren from even a single blade of grass. Were she to tell these irascible townsfolk that this place used to be called the Mojave, they would string her up the same as the young metahuman whom they were tracking. The unmarred (otherwise known as regular, run-of-the-mill humans) posed a certain threat to remnants of the old world on a good day, but en masse, they were an irrefutable force of nature. Hysteria still gripped their feeble minds, jarred by the world around them and what all it could do to squash them out of existence. They had no superhuman abilities to speak of, and many were spiteful for this fact alone. They killed metahumans simply because they were different. Because they were more.

    If she couldn’t find Peck within the next day or so to get a message back down south, then they would eventually have to worry about a recovery team being sent out to find them. It’s not as if her winged friend worked on a schedule… He did things as he pleased most of the time.

    Kara circled back towards the inn and found herself a nice, cozy corner table with an extra chair to rest her feet upon in the meantime. The floor would eventually clear out of ragtag buffoons and she would make her way upstairs in a safe manner so as not to draw attention… but until then, maybe she would draw the soggy brim of her hat down further over her brow and shut her eyes for a spell. The cold always made her tired, after all.

     

     

    “You’re sure this is the place?”

    Alex was irritated. But what else was new?

     “Yeah. I followed them here the last time—”

    The sound of a tin can suddenly scraping across a patch of pavement shot Kara’s gaze over her shoulder. The dilapidated, albeit thick corners of a pre-war parking garage were like squared pillars in the night, tombstones of a once busy galleria. Their sturdy, ramped tiers and painted parking stripes were all that was left. Four floors of pure concrete, at least.

    Alex pressed a finger to her lips and signaled with her other for Kara to follow slowly. She always led the way. Ever the older, protective sister. She turned slightly to whisper without looking back, “How many did you count?”

    “Everyone that petitioned.” Kara replied, mindful of her echoing bootsteps. The first ramp up was just around the next corner.  

    “All of them?”

    “Yeah.”

    “…Damn.”

    Talking those down off a two-centuries-long ledge had proven to be nigh on impossible, unfortunately. It made Kara’s stomach twist into knots just to think of it. To be that far removed from any and all hope whatsoever…

    Hope was the only thing keeping them all going at that point.

    Kara twisted around to look back once more before making the corner. J’onn and the rest were right behind, ready to jump into action at a moment’s notice. There were voices echoing down from the second ramp. They were close.

    “This is so stupid…”

    Kara winced a bit at her sister’s ire, “They’re just tired.”

    “We’re all tired, that’s no excuse.” Alex hissed, both eyes still straight ahead. “Taking the easy way out is selfish.”

    “I don’t think it’s selfish.”

    Alex stopped, and Kara forced down a gulp of air.

    “What?”

    Kara watched as her sister spun around slowly, her brow now shot towards the sky with a look of incredulity.

    “I’m just saying. I think it actually takes a lot of courage to wanna decide something like that—”

    “Would you?” Alex interrupted.

    “Would- would I?”

    Alex waited.

    “I—” Kara stammered, “N-no.”

    “Do me a favor, Kara?”

    “Hm?”

    “Don’t let anyone else hear you talking like that.”

    “Okay.” Kara’s eyes swung low towards the scuffed tops of her boots, “I’m—”

    All of a sudden, something like a very large firework going off inside of an empty grain silo made every head upon the parking garage ramp hit the deck.

    The -boom- that came after was deafening.

    The sound shook the entire building, sending a shockwave right through Kara’s middle and nearly knocking the breath right out from her lungs. She’d clapped both of her hands over her ears…

    …just in time to see the concrete pylon in front of Alex split in half.

    The entire first floor of the parking garage sheared sideways, sloping downward upon its weary remains like a vast sheet of paper thrown onto water. Kara didn’t think. She only reacted.

    “Alex—!” Kara leapt out in front and caught the ceiling as it came down.

    Forced to her hands and knees beneath Kara’s frame, Alex tried to wriggle free.

    It was so sudden.

    So unfair…

    Kara struggled beneath the weight of an entire parking garage level. The jagged ends of rebar above them began to give out loud -twang- sounds as they plucked themselves free of the concrete floor, bent mercilessly from all of the force placed upon them. The floors up above began to drop down on top of them one at a time.

    -slam-

    Two.

    -slam-

    Three.

    “Kara, we need to move!”

    “I can’t—”

    “…Kara!”

    Kara squared her feet and screamed through the hard clench of her teeth. The weight held on top of her shoulders; it may as well have been the entire world.

    “Agh- I can’t!”

    -slam-

    Her eyes yanked themselves open again. Tipped too far forward out of her chair, the wood heel of one of her boots had slipped straight to the floor and jolted her awake again.

    Kara blinked bleary-eyed into the dim, orangish glow of the few oil lamps still lit inside the inn. She yawned and then sat up straight to round out one of her shoulders. Always the same dream. More of an annoyance now than a world-upending realization once she awoke each time. That had long since passed. The pain of it all had spread so very thin; but it never winked out of existence. Not even for a single moment.

    Something moved within the shadows from behind the counter suddenly, far across the now empty floor. There was the sound of a cup circling upon its bottom as if it had been knocked into slightly.

    Kara leaned forward and rubbed her thumbs into the corners of her still-sleepy eyes. “Hello…?”

    A flash of bronze zipped through the open door there towards the back.

    The feathery tips of a folded wing.

    Shot onto her feet like a bullet from a gun, Kara raced across the chair-littered floor and slid hip-first over a tabletop to catch the door from falling all the way shut. She threw herself through it, careful so as not to rouse the innkeeper who was no doubt sleeping nearby.

    “Wait…” She urged with a not-so-subtle whisper, “…wait!”

    The winged figure loped down the long wooden porch and stumbled down the last of its haphazardly built stairs. Kara gained that ground quickly. She ran so fast that she could almost reach out and grab—

    “Leave me alone!”

    “Just wait a minute—”

    “Stop!”

    The boy fought to wrench himself free once they tumbled to the ground, and he gave out a pained yet eerily-avian squawk from Kara’s weight pressing against his left wing— spread now beneath her like a downed mourning dove. She immediately tossed herself back and rocked onto her knees. Kara’s hands came out with a measure of caution...

    “I’m not gonna hurt you.”

    “Get away from me!”

    “Relax—”

    The metahuman boy thrashed the heels of his worn shoes into the snowy slush in an effort to escape her. The look in his eyes was wild. Kara’s heart broke for him.

    “What do you want?!” He seethed.

    “I wanna help you.”

    “You—” The boy huffed, propping himself up and onto his elbows with a bewildered look, “You what?” He looked maybe freshly sixteen or so, pimple faced and greasy haired. The wings that tucked shyly behind his back moved as one with him, though, Kara supposed that they would, being born with them and all. Rare was the metahuman who could not pass as one of the unmarred. Kara sought the special ones out most of all; they were always in the worst of ways.

    “My friends and I- we’ve been looking for you.”

    “Yeah, I got that—”

    “I promise,” Kara held her hands up again once it seemed like he would bolt again, “We just wanna take you someplace safe.”

    The boy’s expression suddenly grew hard, “Why’s that?”

    “Because we’re like you.” Kara insisted, “I can take you to a place that’s filled with kids like you- A place where they’re not scared, or hungry—”

    The boy’s eyes shot open wide with terror. “You’re one of those Children of Liberty freaks,” He scrambled to get back to his feet, “No- no, I’m not going anywhere with you!”

    Kara tried to calm him as best she could, “That’s not—”

    “Hey! Birdboy!”

    An unexpected voice from between two railcars made them both run stiff. Flak-jacketed men with long barreled rifles began to filter out from the nearby tracks, zeroing in on their position.

    Kara tried one last time, “Listen to me very carefully, we need to run now—”

    “I know what you do to us!” The boy screamed. His breath came out in thick, angry clouds, “You’re not taking me! You just want to cut me open!”

    The rapid, tell-tale -pop- of a semi-automatic rang out into the night air right about then, and Kara moved to shield herself in front of the young, frightened metahuman. Bullets aimed low and meant to cripple the boy landed in quick succession against the small of her back, soon followed by the -zip- of a feathered tranq dart. Kara felt around at the perforated material of her thick winter jacket and looked down. She picked up the bent needled end of the dart, showing it flat upon the palm of her hand…

    “No,” Kara shook her head and then gave a toss of her chin over her shoulder, “That’s them.”

    Astonishingly enough, the boy was quick to use the broad length of both of his wings to stand himself up. Without a word, he folded them in to streamline into a sprint. Neither of them spoke again as they ran.

    It was the sheer -whip- of bullets and darts flying past.

    The heavy -thud- of each footfall through a layer of soft snow.

    Breaths, both ragged and frantic…

    The boy at her side hit the ground hard.

    There was the red, billowing tip of a dart stuck straight out from his shoulder. He groaned, drunk-like and eyes lolling to the back of his head quickly, his face smushed into the mud and snow.

    “Shit—” Kara slid to a stop and doubled back.

    It didn’t take long for those chasing them to catch up.

    Kara grabbed the business end of a shotgun as it was shoved near her head, cupping it completely. Once fired, it split into four equal tears down the length of its barrel, like a banana peeled towards its stump. The men that surrounded her stopped on a dime.

    “Remnant—” One shouted, “She’s a remnant! Take her!”

    Kara grumbled beneath her breath. “Great.”

    She threw the shotgun wielding grunt in front of her against the nearest tree some thirty yards away— A pitiful Joshua, gnarled and shrunken in on itself from the harsh and unforgiving winter. It came down with a crash just as a net made its way over the crown of her hat. She fought to get out from underneath it, but the ground beneath her boots had been shuffled free of snow from all of the commotion and was now a quagmire, swallowing up each of her steps.

    Not good.

    Kara snarled with all of her effort to tear the netting which sought to contain her. She hadn’t found herself in such a tight spot in years, not since her trek through Panamanian warlord lands a mere six decades or so ago.

    Something cylindrical landed just beside her in the muck all of a sudden. It gave a tinny -click- and then burst with a foul, green gas. The fog of it all was so thick, it choked her airway instantly. Several more canisters landed lopsidedly all around her after that. Kara felt dizzy.

    Wait…

    She felt very dizzy.

    “M-no.” Kara mumbled. Her lips somehow felt like they weren’t even her own. She flailed with her arms one last time beneath the now very heavy net…

    “…no.”

    There was a -thud-... and then, there was silence.

    It was the strangest thing. Kara felt like going home.

    Chapter End Notes

    Since this is a complete AU, I understand if--at a glance--it looked like something you might be interested in reading, but if it turns out that you're disappointed already or it's just not your cup of tea, then that's okay. I'm sorry to get your hopes up. Feel free to check back in future for other stories that may be more to your liking! I'll admit, I don't ever actually write anything in-universe, because I loved the characters but rather disliked the shows. Sort of commonplace amongst lots of writers, I think. Then you have your writers who do nothing but write in-universe fiction. Everyone's different. Thanks for reading it, regardless. Things will start to make more sense hopefully around the 4th or 5th chapter, so, no hard feelings if you wanna check back around then.

    This one's probably gonna be the grittiest thing I've ever written. Buuuut I freakin' love it already. I have another few days of a lil vacation before work ramps up again, so we'll see how much of this gets put out on your screens by then. K thx love u.

    Chapter 2: Humanity Is a Unicorn

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Two: Humanity Is a Unicorn

     

    It had been so long since Kara had truly felt the weight of her own bones.

    Fused with acidic, experimental compounds and at least quadrupled in density, she moved sluggishly for the first several years after the change… sloth-like, and infuriatingly so. It took several more years after that for her stricter sense of agility to return, but by then, she could walk right into a barreling MBT and stop it in its tracks without earning so much as a scratch. With hair that took special, diamond infused blades to cut and skin that took all manner of assault be it explosive, ballistic, slashing, or stabbing… Kara Danvers was the envy of all armies.

    She was the perfect soldier.

    The rhythmic -click clack- of wheels on track had her lifting her head from whatever cold metallic surface she was lying face down upon. The rattle of chains came whenever she stretched her hands out. The snail’s pace of her movement was nearly equal to her realization— She was in a train car. A moving one.

    Kara groaned as she rolled forward onto her forehead and then lagged slowly into a sitting up position. The tan felt of her Stetson lying beside her was still freckled with dried mud. She remembered the gas, and oddly enough, the smell of the wheat fields that had once grown around her childhood home. Something powerful enough to knock her clean out had thrown her for quite the loop, alright. Smacked her unconscious hard enough to remember what being fifteen was like. Lost, afraid of her body, confused with the world…

    At any rate, she was more curious about how she’d been moved into the long, metal car to begin with. The weight of just one of her hands placed inside both of someone else’s was usually enough to drag them down to the ground, if left unprepared. Kara took a deep breath and sloughed off the rest of her grogginess.

    “Well… crap.”

    She stood up and caught the inside wall of the car as it rocked towards her all of a sudden. A wide turn, but they were going fast. That’s when it hit her: Sara, Ava, and Barry were still back at the inn. If the brutes who had accosted both her and the boy were all on the train with her, then there wouldn’t be any way that Sara could fit together what had happened… Not beyond the obvious spent bullet casings and tranquilizer darts, that is. A sure sign of a struggle. Maybe Peck was keeping up with them? Kara could only hope.

    She began to bang on the walls of the train car, shouting for someone, anyone, to hear her.

    “Hey! Hey- is anyone out there? Where are we going?!”

    Kara pounded the side of her fist near a row of rivets then, which popped loose like buttons on a denim jacket.

    “What did you do with the boy?!” She screamed.

    Unfortunately, she knew who her captors were. The Children of Liberty had a separate reputation apart from fighting to gain one length of railroad track at a time: They kidnapped metahumans —though for what, far too few knew— and most of all, remnants. Kara knew that she would likely be sold to the highest bidder. All in an effort to advance medical science, no doubt. Or, she supposed, into slavery… but at this point, it was anybody’s guess.

    Good luck to whoever tries.

    It wasn’t the first time that she’d been slowed down long enough to be captured. Punching her way out usually did the trick.

    Kara pulled the chains free of her manacles as if they were lazily tied ribbons and tossed them aside. She decided she would just let herself out and walk all the way back to town. However long that would take her. Kara reared a fist back and pounded a knuckle-shaped dent into the container that was her speeding train car— not deep enough to punch all the way through, but knowing that a few more blows would surely do it. It was loud, of course, since the car was empty all except for her.

    The odd -creak- of a small, hinged window at the front of the car suddenly gave way to a lobbed device.

    Another gas canister.

    Kara growled and backpedaled quickly, fists clenched and hanging at her sides. “Not again…”

    Even stranger this time, she remembered the name of their childhood cat.

     

     

    She was admittedly less groggy the second time around.

    At least they hadn’t tried fastening her chains again. It seemed as though they understood what a futile amount of effort that would be, rolling her off of her arms to begin with. Kara peeled her eyes open again and wobbled upright and onto her feet. Pins and needles. How long had she been sleeping folded onto her elbows like that? The train car was still moving.

    “Come on…” She tried, speaking aloud towards the inside of the car, “Let’s be reasonable. Can’t we talk about this?” Kara rubbed along her forearms to wake them back up. She doubted that anyone would answer her, but any information was better than none and that was the only way she would get it. By making noise. “If it’s a trade you want, then I’ve got loads of stuff- much more than you’ll get for me. Let’s make a deal!”

    Being unaware of her location for so long was unsettling, to say the least. There were only so many railways left intact, and only so much length of track that the Children of Liberty owned. Kara knew the amount of heavily armed stations they controlled along each route, and that it would take the rest of the Union weeks to infiltrate each of them in search of her. Hell, she didn’t even know which direction they were heading, whether it was north or south. The Union Pacific ran up the length of the American west coast and was, unfortunately, pretty evenly split between each prevailing power. The CoL had footholds at most of the stops west of the Colorado, but Kara’s organization managed to maintain control of the tracks east by southeast of there, which meant that she had to at least be across the border of what used to be California. The Union would have to push far into enemy territory in order to rescue her. It left a sinking feeling somewhere near her incredibly weary heart.

    The sooner she could escape, the less distance everyone else would have to travel in order to find her.

    Kara began to pace the long rectangular container floor, around and around in search of a weak point. She settled on her remarkably well-placed dent and decided just to tear the rest of the way through as quickly as possible. Whatever awaited her on the outside she would just have to deal with, whether that was flat land or the steep edge of a cliff. Kara took to putting her fist against the thick sheet metal until the bright light of the outside world began to dimple through. There went the sudden -creak- and -clap- of the small window beset inside the door at the front of the car, and then the gas can to follow. Kara tried ripping her means of escape wide enough to breathe fresh air through, but regrettably, she wasn’t fast enough. It apparently didn’t take much of the gas to bring her crashing down right onto her face again. With her last lingering moments of consciousness, she watched the green fog of it whip right through the hole she’d torn like a passing wind…

    …just like the weathervane that sat atop the barn she used to play in.

     

     

    Kara always thought that the sunsets over Laredo were like bathing in the color red.

    The native Spanish Dagger seemed to aid the fire which grew along a pale blue sky each night, propping up bright torches as if to signal the stars. It had not rained in almost a year. People were finally growing restless.

    “This is insane- J’onn, you’re not seriously considering—”

    “They have a right to state their case, Alex.”

    Alex Danvers grumbled to herself as she ground a heel of her shoe into the pebbled dirt. “Yeah, but why does it sound like you’re so eager to side with them?”

    “You only have to listen.”

    “To hell with that!”

    J’onn sighed deeply and bowed his dark brow into the span of his open hand, rubbing at his temples to no doubt quell the frustration between them. Kara admired the man for his endless river of patience. He was, presumably, a great many centuries older than her, after all. Twice now he had witnessed the rise and fall of civilizations, and the ending of worlds— including his own. How did he still have the strength to do anything at all? She pondered this question above all others, for its answer always seemed to elude her.

    “What exactly is it they want us to do?” Kara interjected quietly.

    Alex crossed her arms and then shifted her weight tiredly to face her. “Nothing good. Nothing good at all.”

    Whenever Kara woke up again, she was so parched that her tongue stuck to the roof of her mouth. It was like sandpaper. Swallowing dryly only made her cough until her vision either doubled or blurred. Kara stood up and then furrowed her brow with a level of decisiveness she only reserved for smashing her way through a situation. She’d grown tired of this game.

    “They want to give up, Kara.”

    She could still hear her sister’s voice ringing between her ears. To dream of her was one thing, but to drag her back into the light was another. Kara felt much too close to that pain again. It infuriated her to no end. She stomped over to the door at the front of the train car and shouted…

    “Hey! I know you can hear me!” She abstained from banging against the wall that time. “I need water. I can still die, you know!”

    Her reflexes, thankfully, were quick to return. The small, hinged window in the door came open, but before the canister could make its way inside, she slapped it back through with the palm of her hand and hacked triumphantly at whoever should be standing on the other side—

    “Hah!”

    Seconds later, a hatch above her head came open and she could hear the scream of winter wind as it passed by. Another canister fell inside the car and gave out its awful -click- sound, spewing green fog while it spun around like a top.

    “…Dammit!”

    More of those painful memories came traveling back as soon as she hit the rumbling train car floor.

    “It’s up to us, now.”

    “Alex.” Kara wheezed with tears in her eyes.

    “We have to show them another way.”

     

     

    At least it wasn’t cold anymore.

    Kara was lying on her side; her long blonde hair caught beneath her shoulder. A hand flew towards the crown of her head…

    “My hat…” Kara groaned as she rolled onto her back with a disappointed exhale, “Damn. I liked that hat.”

    Her hands then went to shuffle down the rest of her body. These weren’t her clothes. And this wasn’t her bed.

    “What- what is this?” Kara sat up.

    She looked around at her surroundings and realized that she’d traded the inside of a moving train car for a tall, tiled cell. It was a long room that was split by a row of bars. Plain. Bright. Sterile. A section of the bars looked to be on a track, as if they opened and made a door. There was a toilet and a drain nearby with a spigot hanging above, both likely with running water as they looked well enough maintained. Kara hadn’t seen an environment so clean in decades. She gave a few readying stretches of her neck as she swung her legs over the small, fortified cot she’d been placed upon. There were cinderblocks underneath— strategically placed to hold her weight, she assumed. She had to admit, the bedding was soft. It was nice for a supposed prison. A lot of forethought must have gone into her being there, which was a good sign, if anything. If she were to be killed, then she very much doubted that anyone would go through all the trouble.

    Kara stood up and then cracked the knuckles on her left hand all in a row. She walked over to the wall of bars and thought —if only for a moment— that her escape would take less than five minutes. If her past experiences with capture were any indication, she would simply walk right out and let her new captors blunder after her in vain.

    “Alright.”

    She didn’t so much as pull on two of the bars before a searing, ultra-violet sort of light clapped on in the middle of the room. An eardrum-piercing siren brought her to her knees.

    “Ah—!” She hid her face and covered her ears as tightly as she could, “Stop… stop!”

    The light and the siren immediately vanished. Bewildered, Kara blinked rather blindly into the room again. A voice soon appeared through a speaker tucked into the far-right corner of the room, opposite the bars. It was slightly garbled, as if speaking through string betwixt two empty cans, but Kara could make out the words well enough. It was a woman talking to her as if nothing at all were the matter…

    “Apologies, just a bit of an insurance policy. But, please, don’t try it again. I need to enter the room and assess your state of well-being.”

    “My—” Kara stopped herself short, “…alright.”

    She hated it whenever she was left with no other choice. Things were about to become incredibly violent, and incredibly fast. Kara listened as a series of bolts ran out from the large, padded door on the other side of the bars, which eventually gave a -whoosh- sound as it drew open. Fists at the ready, Kara waited to burst forth from her cage—

    But it was black hair and eyes the color of sparkling jewels that stopped her in her tracks. A slim and rather unintimidating scientist stared back at Kara, unafraid even in the slightest.

    “Comfortable?” The woman nodded towards Kara’s clothes as she stepped further into the light.

    Kara looked down and smoothed her hands over the agreeable gray color of her sweatshirt and matching sweatpants. She even wore socks. Clean ones, too. Bleach white. Kara shrugged, “Sure, I guess—” Then she puzzled her brow in an effort to stop being so goddamned cordial, “Well- no- I mean- but how…?”

    The scientist merely doffed the long white tail of her lab coat away from her hips as she sat in a lone cushioned armchair opposite the bars. There beside her was a skinny, drawered table upon which she placed a small clipboard. She crossed her legs with that continued, silent stare of hers. Kara felt absolutely flummoxed; a rarity in her arsenal of available feelings. She hadn’t felt so befuddled in a very long time.

    The woman’s state of dress seemed so very plain, even for a scientist, but Kara pointed out the accents of her personality quickly enough. She dripped with gold plated jewelry, which only told Kara that she didn’t actually get out much— if at all. A single jade teardrop necklace hung ever so delicately inside the plunge of her ruffled neckline. It was a cream-colored top that looked to be newly manufactured. Impossible, Kara thought… clothes are traded, not made. Even more intriguing, her skin was pale white but beset with the darkest of perfectly placed freckles. Kara felt that her mouth was no longer dry as the desert floor. The woman had apparently been speaking for a while and was looking down the impossible straight bridge of her nose at Kara as if to await an answer of some kind.

    Kara clapped her jaw shut and fumbled for a moment, “I’m sorry?”

    “I was just saying how it took several of us to take care of your street clothes.”

    “That’s…” Kara scrunched her nose at the idea of it, “You mean- you…?”

    The unassuming scientist sitting on the other side of the bars almost smiled. At least, that’s what it looked like at first— one corner of her mouth seemed to turn upward and dimple her cheek a bit. “No.” She replied and then took her clipboard up again, sitting it atop the bend of her knee.

    “What am I doing here?” Kara asked very plainly that time.

    “You’re part of a study.” The woman replied, “My name is Lena. I’ll just need you to answer a few questions first so we can get a baseline going and then—”

    “Wait, wait, wait…” Kara interrupted with a breath buffeted loosely through her lips, “What study? What the hell’s going on?”

    An intently focused Lena looked back up and pinched her brow together with concern, “As opposed to the alternative? One should think that you might be a little grateful.”

    “What?” Kara shook her head, “Okay, I’ll bite. What’s this ‘alternative’ of yours?”

    “What they do with all convicted murderers, I’m sure.”

    “Convicted—” Kara began to sweat beneath the thick material of her collar, “Now, hang on a second—”

    “Tell me, do you possess any regenerative abilities or are you merely impervious to all injury?” Lena asked, clicking her pen.

    “I didn’t kill anyone!”

    “Well, if that were true, then you wouldn’t have been apprehended and placed on trial.”

    “Trial? Lady…” Kara guffawed, “When’s the last time you went outside?”

    Lena finally lifted her gaze again and shifted her hips uncomfortably in her seat. “Those of us who work to make the world a better place choose to do so from where it’s safe, thank you.”

    “Yeah, you’re a real hero. Now let me out of here before I make a mess of your pretty little jail cell.”

    “Again, not a jail—”

    Kara flung herself against the bars, ready and willing to brace the shrill sound of the alarm again, but nothing happened. She gripped the exceptionally well-made steel so tightly that her knuckles began to run white. “I’m serious. Get me the hell out of here.”

    Lena rocked out of her chair quickly and then took a step back, “Careful- you shouldn’t do that.” Her eyes threw themselves towards the piped ceiling, suddenly anxious.

    “Yeah? Why not? It’s not like you’re there to press a button or anything—”

    “No…” Lena shook her head and continued backpedaling for the door. She pointed towards the front of Kara’s cell, “But there are biometric sensors.”

    “What are you talking about? Come back here!” Kara shouted as she reached an arm straight through the bars.

    The burning white light above her cage popped on, and there came the long, shrill tone of the alarm again. Lena had made it through the door just in time. It lasted maybe half as long as the first, and then Lena’s voice was crackling through the intercom again…

    “I warned you. I can’t do anything to stop it if you trigger the perimeter.”

    Kara’s heaved breaths passed through the hard clench of her teeth as she rolled onto her hips. She was mad enough to see red. Her cot came free of its large bolts in the floor and flew across the room to smash against the far wall, crumpling in half on impact. She couldn’t remember the last time she’d flown into a rage like this… Kara’s fist landed against the nearest tiled wall over and over again, but it didn’t so much as splinter or crack. What the hell was it even made of?

    “Please,” Lena’s voice came through like a soft ocean wave against blazing hot sand, “Calm down—”

    “I won’t!” Kara screamed.

    “Okay.” Lena’s reply seemed to weigh heavily with disappointment, “I’ll come back tomorrow. We’ll try this again.”

    “Let me out!”

    The sound of silence was enough to send Kara over the edge.

    “Let me out!”

     

     

    She tossed and turned all night. What she assumed was the night, anyway.

    “Get out of my way, J’onn. I’m going.”

    He’d only meant to help her. Kara wished that she had taken the time to apologize.

    “I am sorry, I cannot let you go out there.”

    “Then come with me.”

    “It is too dangerous, Kara. We would be putting everyone else at risk if we were followed—”

    “I don’t care.”

    ”I do know you well enough to know that is not true.”

    The grief she had felt was thick enough to taste; A heartache so very visceral that her chest felt as if it were caving in. Alex was gone. It took days of digging through rubble to retrieve her body, but the raiders that had been drawn towards the commotion had stopped them before they were able to do so.

    “Do you not think that I have felt your loss as well? I know that pain that seeks to destroy you. I can see it in your eyes. You must not let it overcome—”

    “You can’t stop me.”

    “Yes.” J’onn frowned, “I can.”

    Kara woke up angry.

    The sound of an ink pen scratching into a thin sheet of paper sat her up on the unfamiliar bed, somehow now repaired and pushed back against a wall. She’d almost forgotten where she was. How nice that mere blip of a moment had been.

    The same lab coat wearing scientist was sat there in the chair across the bars. Lena… Kara remembered her name.

    “Have you been watching me sleep?”

    Lena drew her eyes upward with an almost fond sort of gaze into the cell. Perhaps she was too married to the science to allow her feelings on whether or not Kara was a killer to get in the way. Kara didn’t quite know what to make of her yet. It was still too soon to tell. Lena blinked patiently and swapped one leg for the other, crossing it over her knee again. Her calves were bare today. A plain black skirt suited her well.

    “I’ve been monitoring your respiratory rate during sleep cycles, yes.”

    Kara grumbled, “That’s not at all creepy and invasive.”

    “There’s food and water for you.”

    Kara sat up further with that, nearly bolting herself upright. It shook the newly replaced cot almost hard enough to pry it out from the floor again. Kara stopped with a thought then…

    “When did you fix the bed?”

    “You sleep rather hard. That, and our engineers work fast. You don’t remember getting in it?”

    Kara let her jaw travel up and down in search of a clue, “No.”

    Lena looked back down at her clipboard and scribbled a few more notes. “I suppose the incapacitating agent still needs time to flush out of your system. Food may help with that.” She gestured towards the tray of eggs and toast on the floor in front of Kara’s cot.

    Kara leaned down and took up the tray with a mouth-watering sensation. The small breakfast wasn’t enough to restore her completely, but it was better than nothing. Kara ate and drank quickly, speaking politely from behind her hand, “This water tastes a whole lot better than what comes out of the shower…”

    “Flavored electrolytes.” Lena replied as she observed, “I was unsure of how best to resupply your energy. I was only told that your transfer here was a long one.”

    Kara perked up at that. She wiped the crumbs from the corner of her mouth, “How long, exactly? Where are we?”

    Lena pursed her lips as if she were unsure whether or not to answer. Kara only rolled her eyes.

    “Oh, come on, does it really matter if I know or not? Clearly, you’re still under the delusion that I’m a prisoner, so what’s the harm?”

    Lena sighed. “We currently reside in the state once known as Oregon. A city called ‘Sisters’, specifically.”

    “Oregon?” Kara huffed and relaxed her shoulders, “Holy shit.” Then she pinched her brow together, drawing it in its middle to form serious, inquisitive lines, “Wait- if you know what states are, then you must have access to—”

    “A library?” Lena quirked her chin, “Yes.”

    Kara couldn’t believe it. Digital histories were hard enough to come by, but physical ones —especially maps— were even more elusive. Centuries old paper products had an awful habit of deteriorating with even the slightest touch.

    “So, you have books?”

    “Many.”

    “Oh yeah?”

    “Is that so surprising?”

    “Again,” Kara nodded, “You really don’t get out much, do you?”

    Lena seemed to withdraw every time Kara brought up her assumed introverted nature. It wouldn’t serve her well to keep pushing the only person willing to speak with her away, so Kara made the quick decision to bridge that growing gap.

    “What kinds of books?”

    “Archaic medical journals, mostly, and the odd, old-world atlas or two. Nothing that might help you escape, though.”

    “What about novels- fiction?”

    There went that “almost smile” again. “Some, yes.”

    “What kinds?”

    “All kinds. Why, do you wish to read?”

    Kara smirked half-heartedly. “Would love to. Feel free to jot that down in your creepy notes, there.”

    Lena paused for a long moment, seemingly in order to mull around a rather serious thought. Then she took her thumb and forefinger to fiddle with the jade teardrop that hung below her neck. “What might you say to a deal- I bring you a book, and you answer some of my questions?”

    “It would depend on the questions, but I might say ‘okay’.”

    “Alright,” Lena clicked her pen once more and poised to write at the top of her clipboard, “Any requests?”

    “I doubt you’ll have heard of it.”

    “Doubt is an uncomfortable condition, but certainty is a ridiculous one.” Lena replied with a matter-of-fact tone.

    Kara was stunned a second time. “Voltaire?” Her jaw dropped open again, “You’ve read Voltaire?”

    “I’ve collected Voltaire. Repairing old-world print is a sort of hobby- my very small gift to humanity, I suppose.”

    Kara leaned back against the wall where her cot was fixed. She gave a long exhale through her nose, but not so exasperatedly as she meant to. “Humanity is a unicorn. Nice to imagine, but doesn’t actually exist.”

    Lena quirked her chin once more, “I’m not familiar with that one. Who said it?”

    “Me.” Kara shrugged, “Just now, I guess.”

    A small breath of a laugh went through Lena’s nose, and Kara flitted her eyes through the bars of her cell in an instant. She hadn’t meant to be funny…

    Did Lena think she was funny?

    Lena unclicked her pen and stood up out of her chair, drawing her lab coat back to place a hand upon her hip. “What shall I get for you? Last chance.”

    Kara pursed her lips in thought. “The Price of Salt.”

    “Alright.” Lena replied with one foot out the door, “I’ll see if we have it.” Just before the door shut behind her, she twisted to look back through it and bore yet another curious expression upon her face, “One more thing…”

    “Yeah?”

    “What’s a unicorn?”

    Kara was surprised yet a third time that day.

    By some manner of miracle, she laughed.

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading <3

    Chapter 3: Butterfly Wings

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Three: Butterfly Wings

     

    1114.

    1115.

    1116…

    Kara’s fingers were clasped together at the crown of her head, aiding in her upward draw with each sit-up. Her core muscles burned. Her abdominals, glutes, obliques… The frame which they all sat upon begged for continuous activity on account of the burdensome weight they held every day. Should Kara not work out for at least two to three hours each day, then she would do nothing but ache from head to toe. Not ideal, to say the least, considering her small quarters of late. She had to make do where she could.

    “And the age you reached maturity?” Lena’s voice from her comfy chair was like a cool wind over Kara’s beaded sweat.

    Kara huffed with each following sit-up, “The same age as literally every other woman, I guess.” She spied the small camera situated in the top right corner of Lena’s side of the room. It had a small range of lateral tracking movement for whenever she paced her cell, but was idle in its position for at least the past half-hour or so. Kara could only surmise that it followed her every step… but who was monitoring it on the other side for all that time?

    “Do you believe that your aging process terminated around that time, then?”

    “I don’t have any gray hairs, so, maybe. Why does it matter?”

    “Any and all information counts as data. Now, do you feel your true age, or do you feel otherwise?”

    “What does that even mean?”

    “However you understand it, I suppose.”

    “Look, you try getting stuck looking like you’re 25 for eternity and then tell me how you feel about it.” Kara replied with a more flippant tone, “Are you still counting?”

    Lena answered quickly, “1122. Feel free to continue.”

    Kara’s elbows came around whenever she twisted to look back at Lena again. There was the same, unimpressed expression upon her face. She hadn’t even looked up from her notes. Kara tried to hide the frown that played along her lips. Why did she even care? Lena was very married to the science, Kara decided. She knew that it would take a far greater effort than mere physical prowess to get this woman good and off-centered. The small stack of six or so hardbound books near Kara’s bed stared back at her. Maybe their shared intellect was worth a try? She’d read each of them with a voracity she hadn’t felt in a long time. But then again, who could resist Jane Austen and the way the longing writ upon the page leapt forth? Such a thing felt like a myth after so many years surviving a harsh and broken planet. Longing. What the hell did it even accomplish, anyway? Kara had long forgotten.

    “Nah. I’m bored now.” Kara reared to a stop and took one of her sleeves to her damp forehead. Sweat dripped into her eye, and she winced with the salty sting of it.

    Movement on the other side of the room caught Kara’s attention soon after that. Lena was suddenly standing there just in front of the long, floor-to-ceiling bars. She held out a small handkerchief with patient, still mostly unsurprised eyes. Kara noticed that she was standing far inside the biometric perimeter… Set only to Kara’s parameters somehow, it seemed almost like a slap in the face. This place had a strange way of making her feel like an animal one minute, and then like a welcomed guest the next. Lena was obviously to blame for the latter. Kara wasn’t sure how, but Lena was able to disarm her more violent nature with naught but a few words at a time. Kara felt… safe, almost. A flapping sensation in her gut made her think she would be sick.

    Kara got back to her feet and went to reach carefully for the handkerchief. It was the closest they had stood apart from one another in just over a week now. Lena was stunning, Kara had to admit. She was young, and maybe just a little naïve to the rest of the world, but not once ounce of fear seemed to possess her. A dangerous combo, to be sure. It had been too many years to count since Kara had come across someone so unafraid of her.

    “Thank you.” Kara said.

    The handkerchief had come from one of Lena’s more elegant shirt pockets. She’d forgone the stark white lab coat today, as she had done the day before, and the day before that. This had left her arms bare to the bright, fluorescent lighting from up above. Kara saw that her skin was perfect; smooth like polished marble all except for the small cut upon the back of her left hand. A fresh wound, but small enough to be nothing of any real consequence.

    “What happened?” Kara asked, eyeing the cut on Lena’s hand. She dabbed the handkerchief around her temples as well as the back of her neck and watched as Lena’s eyes cast themselves downward, almost as if she were embarrassed.

    “Nothing.”

    When Kara looked closer, she could see a host of other small cuts that had long since been healed. Scars only on the one hand, though not in a manner which seemed to be self-inflicted.

    “Does ‘nothing’ happen a lot?”

    “No, the exclamation ‘oh my god’…”

    “Absolutely not.”

    -clang-

    “I’m just not like you, Alex.”

    -clang-

    “I know.” -clang- “You’re better.”

    -clang-

It came out of her with puled sniffles at first, and then with each tear that tracked down her cheeks to pool against the fabric of her pillowcase. She sobbed quietly, heartbroken that her dreams of Alex were becoming fewer and fewer with each passing night.

    Just when she had gotten used to them again, too.

    Chapter Four: Of Jellyfish and Sabre Blades

    Formulaic down to its footwork, she had her head completely wrapped around the art of the sabre by age nine. Beyond that, lessons were an everyday thing that lasted well into her adulthood. Prescribed by her obscenely powerful father, he claimed that the world was no place for the defenseless. It was kill or be killed. His way of protecting her, she supposed.

    -tack-

    -tack-

    -thwip-

    Her defense was perfectly executed lightning— parry, and then riposte.

    This instructor was faster and less merciful than the last. Lena enjoyed the challenge. Parry four, parry five, riposte— Hit.

Her instructor growled from behind their mask and then broke their stance to loosen their grip for a moment. A hand went through the small, tasseled loop of decorative cord that hung from the hilt of their sabre; a true sign of more frenzied attacks to lay ahead… Lena sighed. This instructor proved to be definitely more ill-tempered, as well. Then again, not many of her more experienced opponents liked the idea of losing to such a fortified defense. Lena won most of her bouts without ever having to advance at all. She reserved that strategy for the day she would need to prove herself, should the need ever arise. For now, her tactics would remain the same. Slashing attacks were beneath her. A well-placed stab was infinitely more respectable.

    “Alright.” Lena said beneath her breath upon her return to en guarde. “Let’s see where you land today.”

    Her instructor was quick to strike several beats against her blade, goading her attack. She wouldn’t fall for it.

    -tack-

    -tack tack-

    -swipe-

    Pushed nearly to the end of their chalked piste, Lena used her opponent’s growing frustration to end the bout quickly. Her parried riposte was a thing of beauty; swiveled from her wrist instead of swung from her elbow, her final hit landed over her instructor’s heart, bowing the slightly curved yet thin metal blade of her sabre. Lena smiled from behind her mask, and her defenses dropped a second later.

    A mistake.

    Angered, her instructor made up the few feet left between them with an explosive running attack, an illegal fleche to land at least one hit…

    …but more so just to cut her.

    -swipe-

    -tack-

    -tack-

A clean -whip- of her instructor’s blade point then sliced the back of her hand, and she dropped her sabre to the floor immediately, catching the blood which poured out from her hand with her other. It dripped upon the tops of her shoes as tears foisted themselves into the corners of her eyes. It hurt.

    Gladdened, her instructor pulled their mask free from their face. There she saw a triumphant scowl.

    “Today’s lesson…” They said, “Expecting your opponent to possess the same level of integrity as you is a mistake. The outside world is not fair. It does not follow the same rules.” With a satisfied huff through their nose, they turned to leave Lena where she kneeled in pain upon the floor, “Clean yourself up.”

    Lena couldn’t believe that they had cut her so deeply. The feeling of failure never failed to instigate her anger. There was always better, faster, stronger…

Metahumans had been the focus of her study for so long, so a true and pure remnant would definitely add the appropriate variables she needed. Lena wished to pioneer the field of regenerative medicine. At her father’s behest, of course, it was the only thing she could think of that might make him proud of her. Her fencing wasn’t enough. Neither was her genius level of intelligence.

She hadn’t admitted as much to Kara, but the gelatinous invertebrates which she spoke of were more of an obsession than anything. Turritopsis dohrnii— the “immortal” jellyfish. A gift from her father, there swam hundreds of them in their two-hundred-and-fifty-gallon tank. Lena loved them more than anything. They were the secret to unlocking the remnant code, she just knew it. Others in her field believed her to be out of her mind, so Lena tried avoiding them at each research station she had lived in over the years. The oceanological base in Stockholm, Sweden where she had turned twelve had put her on the right path, but seeing the world in between her various homes was admittedly short-lived… Her father was over-protective, if anything. She only knew that she liked standing as close as she possibly could to Kara. The danger of it aroused her like nothing else.

    “What?” Kara asked, “Cat got your tongue?”

    Lena quickly snapped herself out of her daze, puzzling her brow then with a question. “Why would a cat—”

    “Never mind.” Kara laughed quietly through her nose.

    “Anyway, you might want to eat before it gets cold.”

    Kara turned up her chin with another laugh. She seemed to do that more and more often. “Oh no, cold food…” She said, shaking her outstretched hands as if she were afraid.

    Lena couldn’t help but to smile at that. Even when Kara was being deliberately facetious, she was funny. Lena sat in her chair while Kara ate and tried prying a few more answers out of her in the interim, but Kara was exceedingly vague as always. She harbored the secrets of her race with a fierceness Lena had never witnessed before. Metahumans weren’t always so well-guarded. They, unlike their predecessors, were more than happy to divulge information on one another if supplied with the right terms. Lena usually sent them on their way once there was no more to be learned from them. If anything, she was just glad to provide them with more comfortable accommodations before they reached their eventual incarceration.

    “Mind telling me today how old you are?”

    “You’re the genius here, aren’t you?” Kara hacked, “I’ll bet you can put it together for yourself.”

    “Sure, I can try, but I’d prefer an actual number for my records, if you please.”

    “How about a ballpark?”

    “…Ballpark?”

    Kara’s mouth dropped open to explain from where she sat at the end of her bed, but instead she let go of her held breath and then stood up to cross the floor of her cell. Lena was even pleased with the way she walked. It was confident, if anything. Or what she assumed to be confidence. Kara was still a sight with just her binder and the rolled-up hem of her gray sweatpants where they sat upon her hips. A sun-kissed sort of tan stretched everywhere the light from her cell touched. Before Lena even realized it, Kara was unraveling the fabric binder around her chest and stepping out of her sweatpants beneath the spigot of her shower. Lena averted her eyes once again, hot now beneath the loose collar of her shirt.

    Kara looked over her shoulder and stopped a moment. “Oh. Sorry…”

    Lena pursed her lips into a thin line as she stared at the opposite tiled wall. “It’s alright. Shower if you need to, I can come back—”

    “But I thought you wanted to talk?”

    “I—” Lena blundered, “Y-yes. Okay.”

    “I forget sometimes… Modesty isn’t exactly a top priority whenever you’ve lived this long. Some things just stop mattering, is all. Other things become more important.”

    “Is that so?” Lena chanced a glance out from the corner of her eye.

    Kara was humming vibrantly into the stream of water that fell over her head. Lena doubted that she’d had many hot showers, wherever it was that she had come from…

    “I hope it’s alright to admit,” Lena gulped down a nervous breath, “…but I find you fascinating.”

    “It’s because I know how to read, right?”

    Lena laughed and shook her head, still mindful of not looking straight on at Kara’s perfectly naked body. “Not just that. I’ve not actually met a remnant before… I grew up with stories, mostly.”

    “Let me guess… the ones where we’re hard and cold and unfeeling, and completely lacking in any kind of humanity?”

    “Roughly, yes.” Lena could feel that classic Kara eyeroll all the way from her chair.

    “Well,” Kara replied, “For what they’re worth, they’re not too far off the mark.”

    “But… you’re not like that.”

    “You haven’t met me at my worst, darlin’.”

    Lena suddenly felt flush with heat all the way up through her neck. It did something both amazing and terrible to her insides, hearing the way Kara called her “darling” but in that very charming, grounded accent of hers. She’d never heard anyone speak in such a way. It was a lot like singing whilst speaking… the heavy, elongated vowel notes and dropping of certain consonants.

    “Why is that, though?”

    “What? That most remnants are assholes?”

    “I suppose.”

    “Imagine having the future you were supposed to have- stolen right out from underneath you. Yeah, a lot of us tried real hard, I guess. To carry on like we were meant to, to have families and homes and to lead the lives that we should have had, but after watching your kids grow up, and then grow old, and then die, and then their kids, and then their kids after that… Everything starts to look a little pointless. That humanity starts to wane a little too thin.”

    “So, correct me if I’m wrong, but it’s just remnants who’re blessed with longevity, right? Their offspring don’t live as long?”

    “Blessed? That’s certainly a word I wouldn’t use. But yeah. Metahumans have what you’d call a ‘normal’ lifespan, should they stay alive long enough, that is.”

    Lena leaned for the clipboard that she usually left in the small drawer of her nearby table. “How does that work, exactly? Having a biologically metamorphosed child?”

    “Well, both parents have to be remnants to start with.” Kara reached to shut off the water finally. “And then from there, at least one meta in the mix to have another generation, but by then it’s a fifty-fifty chance if the other parent is unmarred.”

    Lena sighed as she scratched a few more notes into her lap. “Kara, your DNA would really help me so much…”

    Once Kara was done toweling herself off, she stepped idly into a clean pair of sweats and sat at the end of her bed again, pulling her damp and dripping hair over her shoulder. It was a bronze sort of color when wet, Lena realized. She couldn’t tear her eyes away again.

    “Look…” Kara frowned, “Even if I could give it to you, I wouldn’t.”

    That unbridled admiration suddenly gave way with a slight crack. “Why not? You’d be saving so many lives—”

    “How do I know that, though?” Kara asked, leaning forward with her elbows upon her knees now. “How do I know that you’re not out to do what hundreds of other scientists have tried to do over the past two centuries? Why do you think there are only so few of us left?”

    “You did allude to a mass suicide during one of our conversations…”

    “That was private.” Kara seethed, both eyes narrowed into daggers now. “See? How am I just supposed to trust you?”

    “Haven’t I at least earned some measure of your trust by now?”

    “Cinnamon rolls and borrowed library books do not an eternal bond make.”

    Lena growled beneath her breath and then stood up, crossing her arms over her chest with a quiet harrumph. “When will you stop bringing up the damn cinnamon roll?”

    “How about whenever you start answering some of my questions?”

    Lena squared her feet and stood there a minute longer, lolling her tongue across the front of her teeth to display her frustration. Kara was equal parts sexy as she was aggravating. Honestly, Lena had never felt more alive.

    “Fine.”

    “Alright…” Kara stood up and walked over to the bars, wrapping her hands around them with a slight squeeze between each.

    “What do you want to know?”

    “For starters,” Kara gave a sly smile, “You’ve never been kissed, have you?”

    Lena could almost feel the color drain from her face. She’d expected something a bit more obvious; information regarding the facility’s hierarchy, who might be in charge, or something along those lines… Just not that.

    “I’ll be back later tonight. With dinner.” Lena replied with a sharp turn of her heel.

    Kara didn’t call out after her this time.

    It almost made her wish that she had.

     

     

    “I’m sorry, ma’am, but the Keeper is still in the middle of a meeting—”

    “Listen to me carefully, you can either step out of my way, or I can cripple you out of my way. Whichever you choose makes no difference to me.” Lena raised her brow impatiently, “Well?”

    The sentry guarding the large office door sighed and then hung his head shamefully, “You knocked me out and then let yourself in, deal?”

    “Deal.” Lena piped up with a cheerful tone, bouncing just the once upon her toes. “You may fall down, now.”

    The sentry grumbled again and slowly got down to his knees, laying himself out on the floor as if he’d been rendered unconscious. Lena had to admit, some of her father’s lackeys were smarter than others.

    “Thank you.” She whispered before bracing her hands upon the tall oak door. It burst open dramatically in front of her with a single push, and she stepped pridefully over the sentry to make her entrance.

    Her father, the Keeper of their great research facility, almost smiled. Lionel Luthor shoed the small flock of lab coat wearing scientists away from his absurdly long desk, “Leave me alone now, gentleman. My daughter, the lovely swordfish, wishes to grace me with her presence.”

    Nodding their agreement, the balding, middle-aged researchers shuffled back through the door at the front of Lionel’s office, taking the time each to step over the “knocked out” guard. Lena smiled, awaiting her father’s heavy-handed habit of doting upon her.

    Lionel gave a crooked smile, his nutmeg-colored beard slightly graying now around his chin. Lena wondered for a moment just how many years it had been that level of gray.

    “With how often you toss around my staff, one might think that you actually enjoy it.”

    “Oh, but I do, daddy.” Lena teased. Her father was in a good mood, apparently, and she wished only to capitalize on that good mood with posturing herself as the “perfect” daughter. That, of course, only meant styling herself just as ruthlessly as he himself did… Not her favorite way to spend an afternoon, exactly.

    “Tell me what it is you want, then.” Lionel swiveled around inside of his obscenely ornate leather chair.

    “Why, can’t a girl come and visit her father for no other reason than to spend time?”

    “Out with it, Lena. I really am very busy.”

    Lena dropped all of her pretense then with a huff. “I want to see the towns.”

    Lionel halted the cigar halfway to his mouth, match still struck and lit inside his other hand. “This again…”

    “I know you’re always saying how dangerous it is outside, but isn’t that why we have armed escorts?”

    “Why on Earth would you want to go to the towns? You’ll only disappoint yourself- there is nothing left to find. That’s what our Seekers are for. They know where to look.”

    “This isn’t about work.”

    Lionel swung around in his chair again, facing his daughter now with his cigar halfway chewed into his mouth. His chin-length hair seemed a shade more dull than usual. Lena had always pictured it much more tawny colored. Admittedly, they didn’t see each other as often anymore, despite this research facility actually being smaller than the previous. Maybe she’d not been paying enough attention. It weighed heavily on her heart, that.  

    “Then, pray tell, what is this about?”

    “I just want to see for myself.”

    “Death?” Lionel scoffed, “Murderers? Thieves? Homes made of mud and sticks? Lena, my little stingray, this is why we stay here. There is nothing you need to witness out there that will—”

    “I am an adult. I can leave if I want to.” Lena interrupted. A daring thing to do; interrupt her father.

    Lionel’s expression turned to stone. “No.”

    “No…?”

    “No.” He repeated, “I forbid it.”

    Lena guffawed helplessly, “You can’t just—”

    “Yes.” Lionel slammed his hand flat upon his desk, rattling his whiskey glasses into their nearby decanter. It made Lena leap slightly, frightened now by the sheer volume of his voice. “I can.”

    A second sentry (presumably to replace the one Lena had “incapacitated”) stepped inside the office right about then. His hands were perched calmly atop his weapon, though not so much as to make himself appear threatening. Lena twisted back around to plead with her father, “Daddy, please, just an hour or two? I feel like I’m suffocating—”

    “I would rather you labor to breathe than to wind up dead. The answer is no.”

    Lena nearly stamped her foot in protest. But instead, she swallowed down her anger and decided just to nod a “good day” to her father. She juked a quick advance towards the second sentry on her way out for good measure, who flinched rather embarrassingly where he stood. Lena didn’t have much, but she did at least have a reputation. This she thought about for a long while as her feet took her down one hall after another, until she somehow ended up outside altogether. The snow had nearly melted now, what with their several long days of intense sunshine. The need for a coat was gone. Lena marched over the soggy clumps of grass towards a row of trailers where substrate supplies were kept, until a noise upon the wind brought her to an immediate stop.

    A scream.

    She turned in place a few times. Maybe a dying animal? Their seekers had a habit of hunting just outside the facility. Lena supposed that the sharp increase in temperature had seen the return of all the local game in the area…

    Then there came another scream.

    Eerily human-like this time.

    Lena couldn’t tell where exactly it came from. Her left, or her right, or even if it was up or down somehow. A bird, perhaps? Screeching with another for the ritual of claiming territory? Lena turned around to head back inside, thoroughly unsettled now. Maybe her father was right…

    Maybe the outside world really was full of monsters.
