    Chapter 16: Except for Infiniteness and Latitude of Home

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Sixteen: Except for Infiniteness and Latitude of Home

     

    “I know it doesn’t technically count, but you’ve got my vote.”

    “Thanks, Barry.”

    “Good luck in there.”

    “Hey,” Kara made a hairpin turn before heading inside the Millennium tree, “You wouldn’t mind—”

    “What? Keeping an eye on her?” Barry gave a sly smile as he looked back.

    “I mean- is that alright to ask?”

    Barry replied with a shake of his head. “It’s fine by me, but I get the feeling she might not be super thrilled if I’m just spying on her.”

    “Then try hanging out with her.”

    “Yeah?”

    “Yeah.” Kara smiled, “She’s decent enough company.”

    “Alright.”

    When Kara turned to step inside again, Barry called back to her one last time, “Hey, Kara…”

    “Hm?”

    “Feels like I was just a kid the last time I saw you so happy.”

    Kara laughed and then turned her face away to hide the rush of color to her cheeks. “You were just a kid.”

    How many pages had she turned to get to this point? Instead of thumbing wildly through one after another in search of an answer, the book of her life had once again fallen straight into her lap. It had been so long since Kara had felt anything bordering bashfulness— Her habitually sullen expression had seen to that. A decade-long sourpuss was now a thing of the past, and apart from the look of surprise from each of her friends, she was glad for it. Kara had more reasons of late to enjoy a mirthful frame of mind. Her sister was alive, the Infinite was thriving, Lena was safe…

    …things were looking up.

    There was a commotion of flapping to Kara’s far left once she made her way inside the hollow trunk of their council chamber. The Millennium tree was still such an awe-inspiring centerpiece for their hidden city. Kara remembered their early days of building with as much fondness as she remembered her parents; Though they hadn’t lived to see all of her hard work come to fruition, she knew that they would indeed be proud of her. She was settled upon her destiny to safeguard her father’s world-upending discovery… as well as the progeny that came from its aftermath. It was her cross to bear and no one else’s. Her biggest secret. Her heaviest burden.

    “Ach—!" The sound of Nyssa’s voice cut like a knife, “Shoo, pigeon! Get away!”

    Kara hacked a laugh at the sight of Peck trying and failing to land upon the mouth of Nyssa’s quiver. “You know,” She said, “…he only likes you because you don’t want anything to do with him. Try being nice for once- see if that makes him change his mind.”

    “I have already tried—” Nyssa shooed the hawk away again with long, frustrated sweeps of her hands, “A menace, you are! Really!”

    Two fingers pinched behind Kara’s lips sent out a shrill whistle, to which Peck obliged as a request for him to clear out. Two whistles would bring him back with just as much haste, but Kara had a matter to attend to that didn’t require his keen eyesight or sharp talons. 

    “…Thank you.” Nyssa exasperated, tugging at her cloak.

    There they stood around the large table at the tree’s center with little to no intention of making use of its chairs like usual. Amaya waited with folded arms while Sara and Ava spoke closely with one another, where not too far behind were Cat and Nyssa standing opposite of each other— too proud to admit they were more alike than they were different. J’onn was in attendance as well, though he rarely contributed anything other than his famed sage advice. While he saw himself as a silent overseer, most saw him as this inimitable being capable of settling scores with a mere wave of his hand. J’onn could very well rule with fear should he choose to do so. His extreme age had rounded out the surface of his mentality like river stone. Dulled his perception of time and trivial matters like profit and loss, but his reverence for life stayed intact. He was also the closest thing to a father that Kara had for almost two hundred years.

    “Where would you like to start, Kara?” Amaya began.

    Kara exhaled a nervous breath. “At the beginning, I guess.”

     

     

    Lena enjoyed the sounds of children playing.

    Their pure and uninhibited laughter was the last thing she thought she would find amusing. She sat on a long bench crafted from twisted vines and watched the small sandlot across the Infinite’s lowest valley where a group of adolescent metahumans ran in droves, chasing their burly caretaker in a game of tackle. Mick only smiled during school hours, with the odd exception of his frequent trips to Caitlin’s infirmary, of course. He was smitten with them all. So soft for one so large and intimidating, Lena thought. She might like to get to know him, should he ever stop scowling at her long enough to hold a conversation.  

    The children climbed on top of the towering man every chance they could get, which he would then fall down as if he were wounded. A victorious screech echoed through the Infinite’s earthen dome once, then twice, as Mick leapt to his feet and charged after his flock again.

    “Mind if I sit?” A voice said from behind Lena all of a sudden.

    Lena twisted to look over her shoulder and saw the tawny haired speedster from before, smiling and gesturing towards the open end of the bench.

    “Of course—” Lena shifted to make room for him, “Flash, was it?”

    “Barry’s fine.” He scratched shyly at the back of his neck, “So, penny for your thoughts?”

    “I’m sorry…?”

    Bary shrugged, “Something Kara said once. I don’t know what it means either.”

    They laughed quietly for a moment and then Barry sat with a long sigh. Lena hummed with a quiet admiration for the tranquility provided by their lonely bench, “I’m afraid I have so many thoughts that I don’t quite know where to begin, to be honest.”

    “I take it you’ve never seen so many metas in one place?”

    “Not this many, no.”

    “It took some adjusting at first. Er, that’s what I’ve heard, at least. There was some sort of divide amongst the founders and one of them eventually left, and that was when the Infinite started taking in more than just metas.”

    “You mean sympathizers?”

    Barry nodded, “We take everyone in that we can, but there aren’t many humans that come seeking asylum here anymore. People like Cat forget that, I think. The Infinite’s a well-guarded secret, but there’re places just like this all over the world.”

    Lena frowned. “If you’re worried that I might expose—”

    “No.” Barry waved his hands apologetically, “No, I didn’t mean to imply that. I know you wouldn’t.”

    “Oh?”

    Barry smiled, “It’s just a gut feeling I have. I get those.”

    “I see.” Lena took a deep breath, “It is a remarkable place. ‘Otherworldly’ is a word that I might use- whenever I’m not too busy being speechless, that is.”

    “Hah,” Barry huffed a laugh and then stared upwards, “Otherworldly… J’onn would get a kick out of that.”

    “I’m sorry?”

    “Mm- nothing.”

    “I do have another question, though.”

    “Sure, what is it?”

    “How did you even find it?” Lena gestured towards the rest of the underground city. They both had a perfect view of the Infinite’s layout from where they sat, tucked away upon a level grassy knoll that overlooked the gentle slope of it all. Lena had thought very briefly after first arriving that one could, in theory, place a ball at the entrance and then watch it roll all the way down to the water’s edge.

    Barry laughed again, “Would you believe that Kara walked across it and fell through?”

    Lena gawked playfully, “You’re not serious.” She stared at him idly then, his expression unchanging, “Wait, you’re serious?”

    “Anyway,” Barry chuckled, moving the conversation right along, “It was her, Alex, J’onn, and another remnant- can’t remember his name- who founded it all about a hundred years ago. Now it’s just Kara and Amaya that’s left.”

    “You mean, they’re the only remnants left?”

    “Mhm. Here, anyway.”

    Lena paused, confused. “So, wait… J’onn isn’t a remnant?”

    “Uh—” Barry shifted uncomfortably at that for some reason, “Sorta, but hey…!” He tried changing the subject, “I hear you’re thinking about going to work for Caitlin! That’s great!”

    “Oh, I…” Lena shook her head, “I haven’t exactly—”

    “There’s not always a ton of resources at our disposal here, so every little bit of help counts.” Barry went on, “I will say, though, that if you’ve befriended our local doctor, then everyone else should come around sooner or later.”

    “I’m not so sure…” Lena replied, trying her best to hide her downcast expression.

    In the short amount of time that she had been a guest of the Infinite, Lena felt that there wasn’t much she could do regarding the circumstances of her arrival. The people here were rightfully afraid of her; she had come straight from enemy territory. They avoided her relentlessly. Whenever she turned a street corner, they headed in the opposite direction from her. Whenever she sat somewhere to read from Kara’s library, they gave her a wide berth. It hurt, but Lena understood their mistrust well enough.

    “Barry?” She asked, looking up again.

    “Yeah?”

    “Would you mind walking me home?”

    “Sure!”

    “…slowly?”

    Barry threw his head back then with a playful “ugh” sound, and Lena felt herself laughing out loud again. She liked him. He made her feel less awful about everything.

    Two friends down.

    Only a hundred more or so to go.

     

     

    It was almost as if Amaya had a hard time believing it.

    “And you’re sure you have a clear picture of their defenses? You said you were held below ground level for a matter of months.”

    Kara’s feathers were thoroughly ruffled by now. “Why does it sound like you’re trying to find a hole in my story?” Kara blinked, amazed by one of her oldest friends— and not for the usual reason. Amaya was just as leery as she was stealthy.

    Amaya’s buoyant ringlets bounced back and forth across her shoulders once she shook her head, “One minor detail can result in catastrophe, you know that, Kara. If we’re to follow you into certain danger, then we need- all of us- to understand what that danger looks like.”

    They had been at it for hours.

    Kara had finally sat down.

    She dropped her head into her hands and grumbled again. “The guards rotate every six hours. They have artillery at the main gate and manned towers bracketing the others… I’m pretty sure there’s four in total. There are two holding facilities for metas with different levels of security.” Kara huffed at last, “Lena could tell you all of this herself and with much more accuracy. If you would just let me go and get—”

    “No. I will speak with her.” J’onn said from afar, crossing his ankles to lean against the smooth, thousand-year-old tree trunk.

    Kara’s eyes shot to him in an instant. His own were filled with kindness, just as they always were. She looked back at the group sitting around the table, “I understand the risk…” She insisted, “I do.”

    “How many metas do you think they’re holding prisoner?” Ava asked from across the table.

    “I’m not sure. Lena said they could house anywhere around half a dozen at a time without a lot of trouble.”

    Cat scoffed finally, sitting with her arms folded across her chest. “How much do you really trust this woman, Kara?”

    “With my life.”

    “It’s almost concerning how fast you answered that.”

    “Why?” Kara shot back, “Because she’s human?”

    “That, yes,” Cat tossed her hands into the air, “…and since when was the last time you trusted anyone that much?”

    “You don’t understand what she sacrificed so that I could make it back here to you all.”

    Nyssa tossed her bow onto the middle of the table with that, bemoaning audibly her lot in all of it. “And you don’t seem to understand the lengths that humans will go to in order to capture an entire populace of metas. How can that be? For someone who has lived so long?”

    “She’s not like that!” Kara looked around at the group of stern, still unconvinced faces and guffawed openly, “Come on—” She shot a hand towards Sara, “You saw for yourself, this is Alex we’re talking about. My sister… Your friend!”

    “And what about the people here?” Cat continued. Her expression had softened somewhat by now, hinting at a level of empathy which she reserved only for very few, including Kara. “We would be risking the safety of an entire community if you were to be traced back here—”

    “I know that!”

    Amaya cut in, aware of Kara’s rising anger. “Kara, did you have a chance to talk with Alex? To see her, or even get a message to her?”

    “She…” Kara deflated once again, “No.”

    “What would be her reason for staying there all this time, then? If she’s not being held against her will, then she must, at least, be willing. Do you know why?”

    “I- I don’t think she knows who she is.”

    Nyssa leaned forward at that, “What do you mean?”

    “I mean, she has to know that she’s a remnant…” Kara emphasized, “I dunno, maybe she thinks that she’s safer right under their noses, but honestly, I don’t think she remembers anything else. Even me.”

    It was that last part which hurt to say out loud.

    “You’re sure of this?”

    Kara dropped her head all the way down to the table, giving it a thick -knock- sound with all of her back-and-forth blustering, “Like I said,” She lamented tiredly, “I’m not sure of anything except for the fact that she’s alive.”

    “Kara…” It was Sara’s mellow and compassionate tone of voice to hinder most of the yelling then, which was an odd thing for sure, since their headstrong pilot was almost always one to lead with her emotions. “Can I ask you something?”

    Kara lifted her head again with bleary eyes, “What?”

    “What if we get there, and Alex doesn’t want to leave?”

    Kara almost laughed, but it was her realization that Sara might actually be right which stopped her short. “That won’t happen.” She replied, firm and resolute.

    “How do you know?”

    “Because I won’t let it happen.”

    “…Kara.”

    “I can make her remember!” Kara yelled again, looking around with wide eyes. She felt herself bordering upon a frantic state. The reality of her situation was slowly sinking in. Kara was feeling her sister slip away again. “What are we even talking about anymore?” She stood up, “You know there’s only one choice!”

    Sara stood along with her, both hands held out in surrender. “Hey, I’m with you, okay?” She replied with a smile, “Nothing short of a natural disaster’s gonna stop me from coming along.”

    With that, Amaya clasped her hands and then bowed her head for a moment, reverent as always. “Alright. We’ll take a vote.”

    Kara sat down again and shifted upon the sturdy legs of her chair, feeling as though she might be sick. The air inside their council chamber felt charged for all of the suspense hanging within it, and Kara suddenly wished to be anywhere else in the world at that very moment. Or perhaps, just one place… One very specific place.

    Lena’s breathy giggling was like a song on the wind. “Oh, totally- sure you could.”

    Kara pulled her knees in tighter where she lay opposite Lena, their campfire crackling with a small -pop- beneath all of the tall pines. “What?! I can be sneaky when I want to be!”

    “I believe you.”

    “Seriously!”

    “Mhm.” Lena nodded with a close-lipped smile from ear-to-ear, “Whatever you say.”

    “All those in favor of going after Alex, raise your hand.”

    Rocked back to the present, Kara gulped down a breath and then struck her own hand towards the grand canopy. One by one, the rest of the council began to follow.

    Sara.

    Ava.

    Amaya.

    Cat kept her eyes glued to her lap, almost as if she were ashamed, but that was where her hands remained. Nyssa would surely begrudge herself long enough to finally cast her own vote, but Cat’s lack of action was a shock, indeed.

    “Cat.” Kara drawled, “…What are you doing?”

    The forlorn woman didn’t dare to look up. “I’m sorry, Kara.” She whispered.

    Kara began to sputter helplessly, “You- you can’t just expect me to think that you’re fine with leaving Alex in danger?” Silence filled the space between them, and Kara pressed her once more, “Cat…?”

    Finally, Cat lifted her gaze. She seemed purely remorseful upon the heels of her decision, but that didn’t assuage hardly any of Kara’s broken heart.

    “It just seems to me that she’s not actually in any real danger.” Cat explained, “She’s been there for so long already- have you stopped to consider that it might be the only place she calls home now?”

    “I… I can’t believe you—”

    Sara chimed in again all of a sudden, that compassion in her voice now replaced with a stinging spearhead, “Nyssa.” She hissed, “Raise your damn hand.”

    Nyssa responded with an equal amount of venom, “You pull my strings no longer, love.” She then gave a solemn nod in Cat’s direction, “She’s right.”

    Kara brought her hand down flat upon the table with a loud -crack- sound, nearly splitting the wood. “Think about what you’re doing! It’s not just Alex who needs our help—!”

    “Exactly, Kara!” Cat shouted back, “These people- these people here need our help!”

    Sara stood up, shirking away from Ava who reached out to hold her back. Her long sandy tresses curtained her face once she leaned far over the table, jabbing a finger towards Nyssa and angry enough to flush red over her slew of fine freckles, “I don’t know why we ever let you into these meetings.”

    “On that, we agree.” Nyssa replied.

    “We need a unanimous vote! What is wrong with you two?!”

    Nyssa crossed her arms, “You would leave this entire city vulnerable to attack just for—”

    “For my sister.” Kara spat, tears gathering quickly into the corners of her eyes, “We built this place so that people like you could feel safe, assassin.” She dragged her hurt expression back towards Cat who was staring pitifully into her lap again, “Cat…” Kara begged, “Please.”

    Another moment of silence rolled through the hollow chamber like a rumbling earthquake. The quietude of it jarred everyone. The steady upheaval sought to tear Kara in half.

    Feeling that the matter was closed, J’onn hung his head with a downcast breath and then walked out through the grand tree’s exit.

    Kara’s world was upended. Again.

    It wasn’t long after that when Sara seemed to bear the sound of cricket chirps no longer. She drew her batons from their perch between her shoulder blades and then slammed them down upon the table, matching her ex’s infuriating doggedness…

    “If it wasn’t for people like us- then there wouldn’t be anyone left to look after. Don’t you at least get that? The only thing worth anything in this life is the people you share it with, and those people are here now.” Sara jumped slightly whenever Ava reached to lace their fingers together. She sat there quietly, looking up towards Sara with so much adoration in her eyes. Never was there a love truer than theirs.

    Sara took a moment to swallow down the brunt of her fury and then gave a sure nod across the table. “You’re gonna wake up one day and hate yourself for this. I promise you.”

    Cat’s sad eyes made their way towards Kara one last time. “I… can’t lose you again.”

    Not one for sticking around once damage had been dealt, Kara stood up so abruptly that her chair toppled over behind her. She rounded out of the tree’s exit with the weight of the world crashing down upon her shoulders. Her neck ached and her heart pounded, but as soon as she was amidst the lower subterranea of her cherished city, the dull drone of her ringing ears subsided, and she was alone with her pain again. Kara walked aimlessly down one dusty road after another, looping around prickly stands of agave and chittering scurries of chipmunks. The city was alive, but Kara felt dead inside.

    Most of the pueblo windows were shuttered here on the lower level. They were boarded up for safe keeping, waiting for occupants to light their cobwebbed lanterns. There was always room for more.

    Kara thought back to a time when Alex doubted whether or not they would have enough space for everyone… The Infinite was a safe haven for metas, but it was also a passing place for those in need of a brief respite. Its secrecy was paramount. Their one cardinal rule…

    To keep them safe.

    Tormented still, she kicked at the pebbles which lined the road in front of her. Kara couldn’t shake her mind free of its shackles, no matter how hard she tried. The doom her sister faced should she be found out… Kara had known the possibility of objections going in, but she had put so much more effort towards the act of convincing them all to go along with it. Surely, her council of peers would have understood, and they did, but the risk ultimately outweighed the reward. There was no one to be angry with. That was the worst part. Alex was left to hang in the balance as a result. Kara reminded herself again that the decision wasn’t made to spite her. Cat did have a point. While blanketed in selfishness, it wasn’t any less true that the safety of their city fell into the hands of the “greater good” just as it always did.

    Should she wait fifty years and try again? Forty? Kara didn’t exactly need the council’s blessing to go it alone— but she did need their help. On her own, breaking into a heavily fortified complex and rescuing Alex was nigh on impossible without suffering many human casualties. The only way to avoid any potential loss of life was with a full team.

    A team she didn’t have.

    Barry was undoubtedly in. So were Sara and Ava… but Amaya would certainly toe the line as far as what was best for their people.

    The front of Kara’s home stood upon a steep slope with a winding path outlined by limestone pieces. She stared with glazed eyes as her feet took her closer to her door. None of it felt real.

    What if Sara was right?

    What if Alex has been mad at me this whole time?

    What if she never wants to come home?

    Kara hung her head dejectedly and then leaned her brow against the smooth surface of her door.

    Does my sister hate me…?

    Regardless of the reason, the thought of leaving Alex behind was almost worse than originally thinking her dead. It was so beyond unfair. Would she eventually find enough people willing to risk their lives for one single remnant? When would be a good time? Worse yet, would there ever be a good time?

    Kara could hear the sound of Lena’s voice coming from inside, her usual soliloquy when either solving a problem or reading to herself. For being so close yet so far, Kara realized how much she missed her.

    Sara’s words began to ring between Kara’s ears like a distant echo, and the shockwave of her next realization nearly made her stumble the rest of the way through the door. It was as if Kara suddenly understood the one thing that had eluded her all these years… For the first time in a long time, everything was so very clear.

    Emily Dickinson had written it perfectly.

    Forever—is composed of Nows.

    Kara took herself inside and rounded the first corner towards her sitting room. There was Lena, sat cross-legged upon Kara’s sheepskin rug with her back against the stonework fireplace. In her lap there was a book, splayed open about halfway through.

    “Kara…?” Lena looked up, somewhat startled.

    Kara just stood there, speechless. Some divine hand or other had led Lena straight to Kara’s most treasured novel; a story which she had cherished for centuries and shared with no one else. The one she had asked for months ago while still in her stark white cell. The very book that had built the bridge between them to begin with. Kara blinked. She didn’t know what else to say other than what lied in print there between Lena’s hands.

    “I feel I stand in a desert with my hands outstretched, and you are raining down upon me…”

    She felt the words come out of her mouth as if they were gospel, spoken with the guidance of angels both devastating and wonderful. Lena stood up and clutched the book to her chest, which Kara moved across the room to stand with her. She wished to touch her, to sing her praises.

    “…I will comb you like music caught in the heads of all the trees in the forest.”

    None other than The Price of Salt by Patricia Highsmith had ever described the feeling of happiness with such accuracy; The silent crash of two clouds did truly seem almost more than Kara could bear. She reached to pull at Lena’s waist, and a moment later they became inseparable with small whimpers of joy passing back and forth between their lips.

    The book eventually flopped onto its spine near the rug where Kara had laid them down. She didn’t care for how brittle its pages were after so many years— she had read it a thousand times from cover to cover and had memorized its every paragraph, and every faded punctuation mark. It could stay in the past, Kara decided. Lena was her present.

    “I can’t keep myself from you anymore.” Kara mouthed gently beneath the lobe of Lena’s ear, shuddering amidst her quiet mewls and gasps, “I have to love you before you’re gone.”

    The fervent moans Lena caged inside of her mouth grew more and more desperate with every inch of her body that Kara managed to claim. She writhed against the soft sheepskin as Kara’s lips passed wetly along her neck, “Oh, Kara—”

    Kara shuddered again, “…God.”

    Lena mewled sweetly once more and searched around blindly for one of Kara’s hands. She found it and then begged for it to press hard between her legs, “More than anything,” Lena whined, “I want more than anything for you to love me.”

    Kara held herself aloft on her elbow and then spread her knees apart, taking Lena’s along with them. The affirmative nodding against her brow was so innocent and heartfelt; Kara felt that she might sob for how good it felt to be desired so terribly. That she might allow herself to feel that same, terrible desire, and that she might give herself completely just as she would take…

    The skin of Lena’s waist was the softest thing to grace the inside of her palm in so long— her fingers soon slipped past the feeling of denim, which raked scratchily over her knuckles towards the warmth that lied just a little further below. Lena’s head suddenly rolled back against the cream-colored wool, and she keened blissfully from behind the bite of her lip. She was just as wet as she was sensitive.

    Every swipe from the pads of Kara’s fingertips made Lena bow upwards with little cries, anticipating her soul to fling straight out from her body as if ejected right into space.

    “Mm- mm…” Lena whimpered, stopping only long enough to catch her breath in little, rhythmic pants. Her head dropped back to the rug again, and it was as if she was over the moon, lost to the swirling tempest of gaseous clouds and starry clusters. The swan-like curve of her neck was tracked with the most beautiful bulge of bluish veins, veiled beneath her perfect, porcelain skin. Her laryngeal prominence bobbed helplessly with the last of her euphoric moans, “Hm, hmm, hah—”

    Lena’s legs suddenly clapped shut around Kara’s waist, and Kara groaned. The sharp buck of her hips was all too telling. They went straight upward, over and over like the repeated -crack- of a whip. If Kara could indeed be injured, then Lena’s fingernails surely would have drawn blood, scoring small half-moons in a straight line down the back of her neck.

    Lena heaved one breath after another and her eyes fought to open once again, half-lidded and almost drunk for what her body had done.

    “Shh.” Kara hushed against her cheek, kissing there with small, loving pecks. She kept her hand as still as possible over the slick of Lena’s still-throbbing cunt. She dared not to move and bring an end to their tangled mess… If only she could remain there, safely crashed upon Lena’s shore.

    The day had been long and devastating, but one thing was made abundantly clear thereupon the quivering brow of Lena’s blissed expression. Lena was most certainly Kara’s undoing, but she was also her inevitable restoration. All of their pieces fit, and in that instant, Kara felt herself made whole.

    Better, even. Simply for having known her.

    Chapter End Notes

    Slow steps still for our lovebirds.

    If you have not read "The Price of Salt" then I would highly encourage you to do so. I enjoy it at least once a year. A classic, for sure. It's been a bit again since my last update, and it's looking like my future updates will stay that way for the next little while. I may have to put this story on hiatus, but I haven't decided anything yet. I'm working so much and am still so tired and uninterested in most things.

    Chapter title is another Dickinson snippet. I love her so much. Thanks for reading.

    Chapter 17: Battleborn

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Seventeen: Battleborn

     

    Lena was thinking about her jellyfish.

    No larger than the size of the nail upon her pinky finger, she knew they were quite helpless without her. Not in the way that they might die of starvation or perish from any other outside influence… No, they would simply shed their greater parts and revert back to a polyp stage, doomed to repeat, again and again, and again…

    …just like Kara.

    She tucked her knees closer to her chest where she sat on the verdant lawn south of the Millennium tree. The very ends of its branches still reached so far. All of Lena’s insisting that she go along inside to appeal the council’s decision had failed, but she thought to stay outside anyway just in case they might need her. At least, she hoped that they might. Was her part in it all really so insignificant? She only wished to help, but no one other than Kara and J’onn had so much as spoken to her at length about it.

    The sun had hung high in the east when Kara had gone in that morning. Brilliant, diagonal beams of bright yellow moved from one side of the tree canopy to the other, shining down inside to grace the Infinite with daylight… But after so long, Lena knew that the day would soon be over. She eventually resigned to lie back upon the lawn and to close her eyes, if only for a moment. There they swam again; the small, bluish translucent creatures she had once called her friends, spotting the backs of her eyelids with a fond “hello”.

    Father wouldn’t get rid of them…

    Would he?

    With a huff, Lena clasped her hands beneath her cheek as she rolled over to rid herself of the thought. It was uncomfortable laying on her side, so she lied flat on her back again and blinked upward into the wide tree canopy above. A low branch with a swinging boot soon caught her attention— black leather and red dirt. It was the glint of steel that made Lena gasp for breath. There it was as plain as day; the unmistakable hilt guard of her sabre rolling back and forth between a pair of hands. Someone in a hooded cloak sat there high in the tree as if it were any other afternoon. They pawed at her sword with an ill-mannered sentiment, lacking the respect such a finely crafted weapon deserved.

    Of all the nerve…

    Lena bolted upright and jawed openly for a moment, at a loss for words. If someone wanted to see her sabre, then why couldn’t they have asked?

    “Excuse me!”

    A pocket of air just offset from Lena’s shoulder suddenly collapsed in on itself with a dull -pop- sound, emitting a black, shadowy mist in its wake. Lena startled at it and scuttled away upon the heels of her hands and her hips, and when she looked back up into the tree, she saw that the cloaked figure was gone. All that was left was more of that eerie, effervescent mist. A voice broke the silence not a moment later, as if someone were standing directly beside her now.

    “Tell me…”

    Lena gave a small squeak of surprise, “Oh—!” She got to her feet quickly after that and then clasped her hands over her heart, afraid that it might all but leap from her chest.

    “How did you really come by this blade?”

    “I—” Lena fought through the slowly drying mud of her mind to remember the person’s name, recognizing her now as the dark-eyed archer. “Nyssa, right?” She asked nervously.

    “I find myself with so many questions, lately.” Nyssa balanced Lena’s sabre perfectly upon two of her fingers, staring at it lengthwise with a severe sense of admiration. It was either that, or disdain. Lena couldn’t be sure. Nyssa spoke again, her honeyed tone just as misleading as the shadowed look in her eyes, “I like knowing where things stand, but more so- where things belong.”

    “I’m sorry?”

    Lena flinched as soon as Nyssa lowered the sword to gesture towards the trunk of the Millennium tree. “For instance, I find comfort in knowing that even though the acorn falls, it still belongs to the tree.”

    Lena stammered a bit more, but was still so awestruck at the fact that the woman had just teleported in front of her like it was nothing. It was as easy a thing as walking was to an able-bodied person. Nyssa crept slowly around Lena, walking a tight circle supposedly to size her up. People had been doing that a lot; assessing her. Lena cleared her throat quietly in an effort to make herself less intimidating, though, she doubted very much that Nyssa could be intimidated at all. By anyone, or anything. Even the woman’s measured calmness was intense. Lena decided to reach slowly for her sabre…

    “I should like my sword back,” She blinked, “…please.”

    With the nimblest of reflexes, Nyssa snatched it away in an instant. “Why?” She asked.

    “Because. It’s mine.”

    “No, it isn’t.” Nyssa finally drew her hood back to rest floppily upon the tops of her shoulders. “Haven’t you been listening?”

    Of all the sour, pinched faces that Lena had weathered during her most formative years; The ones who had sought to make her feel poorly or disappointed in herself for answering a test question incorrectly, or overly emotional at times due to her growing sense of loneliness… Nyssa’s belittling countenance reached far past any of that hurt. The way she looked at Lena with those piercing and irreverent eyes, as if she were better, or simply more evolved. Lena knew that she was.

    “Give it back.”

    Nyssa sighed and shook her head.

    Lena was presented with a question in that very moment. How far was she willing to go in order to avoid conflict? She stood there and clenched her jaw tightly, deciding on one way or the other. Lena realized that her state of neutrality in this city would eventually begin to show its cracks— for no amount of her claiming to be on the side of metahumans would ever outweigh the truth of her origins. She would need to prove her worth, or at the very least, prove that she could stand on her own in the face of an opposing force. One much greater than her own, at that.

    She sent a quick lash of her arm out to grab at her sword a second time, but there went a passing -fwip- and following -pop- that came from behind her in response. Nyssa smiled as Lena spun around, displaying egregiously proud dimples to rival that of her own. She was beautiful, yes, but also extremely dangerous… if not a little infuriating.

    “Aren’t you supposed to be inside listening to Kara’s appeal?” Lena vexed with another mad leap for her weapon. Nyssa dodged once again with a fluid backwards step.

    Finally, something Lena recognized.

    Nyssa’s footwork was painfully obvious. Like a cat toying with an injured mouse, she was too busy enjoying herself to notice Lena sizing her up in return as a prospective opponent. Nyssa stood with an evenly spaced stance, with her weight held onto the balls of her feet and both knees bent…

    “Appeal? Oh, that. Yes.” Nyssa raised a dark eyebrow, “We adjourned hours ago, my dear.”

    Lena stopped. “…What?”

    “Goodness, have you been waiting all this time?” Nyssa asked with another sly grin. She sucked at the backs of her teeth with a pitiful -tsk- sound and then feigned a pout. It sent Lena’s blood pressure skyrocketing.

    -fwip-

    -pop-

    -fwip-

    After swiping another two times and coming up empty, Lena stamped her foot angrily. She swung her arms through one of the many black, vaporous clouds hanging about in a listless manner, swiping all of it away again so that she could see, “Nyssa—!”

    -pop-

    “Yes?”

    “Ach!” Lena startled again and covered her mouth with a hand, embarrassed for the sound that had come out of it. Nyssa was standing right in front of her now, wide sneer of a grin ever present.

    So…

    This is where we draw a line, is it?

    Lena felt the muscle at the back of her jaw bunch into a small hill whenever she clenched her teeth.

    Fine.

    Instead of reaching for her sword, and without looking down, Lena took a fast step forward and pinned Nyssa’s foot down with her own. She wouldn’t be going anywhere. Not this time. Before Nyssa realized it, she found herself flailing backwards and with both arms windmilling in an effort to keep herself from falling. The spare millisecond she spent with her surprise left Lena an opening to follow through with her own weight— one knee landing against another and with her hand latched upon the handle of her sword. As soon as Nyssa hit the ground, the blade came out clean from its scabbard with a steely -fring- noise. It was still as sharp as the day it was forged.

    Victorious, Lena sent a smile back down at the dark-haired cutthroat. All of her father’s talk of treacherous metahumans began to crash at the forefront of her mind, but still, she was unafraid. Stubbornly, so. Those stories had worked to instill a deep-rooted fear whenever she was a child, but they had somehow failed to keep her curiosity in check. Standing there with her sword in hand again and having gained the upper hand so flawlessly over one malcontent such as Nyssa… What was there to be afraid of?

    Such a graceful display of skill seemed to thrust an angry color into Nyssa’s cheeks. She scraped herself back onto her knees and then to her feet, dusting her cloak of acorn hats and uprooted blades of grass.

    “I did ask.” Lena admitted plainly, pointing the tip of her blade skyward.

    “That does not belong to you.”

    “Yes,” Lena dared to lean forward, seething emphatically through her teeth at the other woman, “…it does.”

    She would make herself understood here and now, whether this former assassin liked it or not.

    An irate brush of Nyssa’s hand swept the tail of her cloak away, revealing a curved dagger there at her hip. She took it from its leather sheath with a growl, “Then prove it.”

    Lena wouldn’t so much as get the chance to do that, not with a voice like the low boom of thunder stopping her right in her tracks.

    “Enough.” It said.

    Whoever it was had spoken so loudly and from all around them that it dropped both women straight to their knees.

    Lena clapped her hands over her ears once each of their weapons hit the ground. She felt that the voice had come from the middles of her teeth, somehow, or as if it had spoken to her from the dark, hidden depths of her mind. Bewildered, Lena looked back up at Nyssa and saw that she had felt it the very same, but instead of astonishment, there sat a look of mild annoyance upon her face. A gust of wind came in with the landing of feet, and suddenly there was J’onn standing between them… He wore a frown and squared it solely in Nyssa’s direction.

    Struck speechless, Lena watched as Nyssa’s mouth grumbled around a few choice words and then as she got to her feet again, stomping in the opposite direction. The broody archer popped out of sight with one last cloudy black huff at the water’s edge, and then she was gone. Lena’s chest was still heaving up and down, having all but forgotten that her hands were still cupping her ears. J’onn turned around to take them from her as an offer to help her stand.

    “I apologize for Nyssa.” He said, “Many claim that she is an acquired taste.”

    “Like soured milk, I’m sure.” Lena replied, brushing some of her hair from her face. The feeling of being constantly shocked around every corner here was beginning to grow old; it was far easier just to roll with the punches as they came.

    The sound of J’onn’s laugh coming from his belly was a welcome relief to Lena’s ears. It was nice just to let her guard down for once. Only being able to do so around Kara seemed to exhaust her as the days went by… Kara had been hounding the Infinite’s council like that persistent dog she always spoke of, anxious to get after a bone and to chew to her heart’s content. Doing so left Lena with almost too much time to sit around and think. Kara came back to her homely pueblo most evenings with shoulders that drooped, and a stare that never seemed to pull up from the floor. They talked, and then they kissed, but neither of them seemed to be able to move past that profound sadness that hung stagnant between them. Kara’s heart was shattered.

    “I suppose she’ll just take some getting used to.” Lena replied after a while with a shrug, “But at the same time, I don’t exactly know how long I’ll be here, so my upsetting everyone may be relatively short-lived. If that helps.”

    J’onn frowned again. “It doesn’t.” Lena looked up into his eyes. Bold and earnest, they were, as if he meant every word of what he said. “You are welcome here.”

    “Thank you. You’re very kind.”

    “Truthfully, I think a lot of the people here have lost sight of what a peaceful life truly looks like.” J’onn explained, “It is a hard thing to find again, once lost.” He took a deep breath and then looked around, admiring the small dole of doves that cooed warmly from the Millennium tree’s low, swooping branches. “Being battleborn does that to a soul. Rips it in half.”

    Lena bent down to retrieve her sword and then sighed, cherishing the solitude that the man had brought with him. “So, you believe in souls?” She asked.

    “Something like that, at least.”

    “Pardon my asking,” Lena shook her head briefly, “I’ve just not met anyone who has any real faith in God.”

    “Now, I didn’t quite say that.”

    “You…” Lena’s jaw traveled up and down again, then she nodded, “I see. My mistake.”

    “Don’t worry about it. Besides,” J’onn leaned forward with a patient wink from his eye, “I was here long before kings and rich men alike made ‘Him’ up to begin with.” Breezing right past Lena’s look of surprise, J’onn continued, “But I am glad to finally have another chance to speak privately with you. There is a favor I might ask of you. One that you might ask of yourself, really.”

    “Oh?” Lena blinked, still astonished as the day was long, “Anything- yes, of course. What can I do?”

    J’onn took her gently by the shoulders and then looked straight into her eyes. “You can forgive yourself.”

    “I…”

    He can’t mean—

    How- how can he know?

    “I don’t know what you mean.” Lena lied.

    I don’t understand…

    I haven’t told anyone. Not even Kara.

    “I think you do.”

    The guilt-laden tremor in Lena’s hand suddenly returned. She could still feel the sinewy, slicing plunge of her sabre blade betwixt a wrenched pair of shoulder blades. Even the sound it had made whenever it had been pulled free… It sent a shiver up her spine just to hear the echo of its memory again.

    “How…?” Lena asked, lip quivering. The tears in her eyes held a fierce sting to them, making her wince for how quickly they had appeared.

    “I feel your regret.” J’onn replied, “You should know how rare of a quality that is, child. Most who kill are not as ashamed of it.”

    Lena choked past a sob, immediately flinging herself towards an explanation, “I didn’t mean to do it—”

    “Then forgive yourself.”

    “I…” Lena sniffled and then shook her head, “That’s the problem. I don’t think I know how.”

    J’onn squeezed the tops of her shoulders and then pulled her close, hugging her tightly. “I trust that you’ll find a way.”

    Lena stood there inside of his large arms, listening to the welcome beating of his heart. She wasn’t sure if it was the thundering of her own which confused her, but she could almost swear to have heard six valves working together instead of the typical, humanoid four. Lena sighed and rolled her cheek against his chest, “I still don’t see why anyone had to die at all.”

    “Yes,” J’onn deflated a bit, “I have been saying this for nearly ten millennia. So far, no one has listened.”

     

     

    The wind against her cheeks was pure bliss.

    Having given herself more freedom than she had ever had in her entire life, Lena decided that she loved motorcycles.

    Especially Kara’s.

    It sat low to the ground and bore a wide seat large enough for them both. The rear tire was fat, and nearly the width of the machine to begin with. Besides the fact that she’d never seen a vehicle this old to still operate with any real success, it carried Kara’s weight like the gliding wing of an eagle. Lena supposed that Kara wouldn’t be caught dead riding a hoverbike, anyway. The v-twin engine which chopped with each pull of the throttle suited her more than anything.

    They had been riding for a while now.

    It was almost impossible to speak over the roar of the bike at a decent speed, so Lena was content just to hug Kara around her middle until they reached their destination. They leaned left and then right many times, until finally they came to a slow crawl, and then a stop.

    Lena smiled as she fingered through her mussed hair and all of its tangles, combing it back and over her ears again. Her cheeks tingled for all of the warm, summer wind that had kissed them over the course of their trip. Kara kicked the stand down for the bike and gave an appreciative tap with the toe of her boot against its bulky, reinforced frame.

    “Where exactly have you taken me, handsome?” Lena reached to palm lightly at the prominent end of Kara’s jaw.

    Kara’s hair had been cut just the day before, and only then did Lena realize that she missed the long, golden braid that reached nearly to the small of Kara’s back. It was shoulder length now but still as wavy as ever. Kara bowed forward and gave a kiss to Lena’s temple, “Come and see. I’d like to introduce you to a few people.”

    Lena bounced lightly onto her toes, clasping her hands in front of herself, “Alright.”

    They had passed several urban areas on their way there, all unsound ruins, of course— war torn and with makeshift battlements each. Here upon the sandy, thornbush scattered countryside, signs of civilization were few and far between. It was a small community of tumbledown structures, decayed and stripped down to their very bones. Roofs fallen in and cornerstones bared, Lena assumed the town was once a lively hub for trade-skilled people. Dilapidated farming equipment laid strewn about everywhere as evidence, picked clean for their parts. The place looked to have been ransacked at least a dozen times. Lena felt confused… How could anyone live here?

    Kara took her by the hand and led her towards a tall oak tree which sat nearby. There were plenty of them just like it in the brush country, but this one held an unspeakable sort of presence. They stepped around its wide, gnarled trunk, and Lena felt herself gasp…

    Tombstones.

    Dozens of them.

    “Don’t be alarmed—”

    “I’m not,” Lena shook her head with a throaty swallow, “I just see now why you had a hard time describing where we were going.”

    “How do you feel?” Kara asked quietly.

    “Sad, I suppose.”

    “Well, I’m happy that you’re here.”

    Lena turned to face Kara again, “Really?” The corner of her mouth stretched upwards into a smile, and she felt settled again.

    Kara nodded and took a step closer. “You’re important to me.” Then she gestured with a wide sweep of her hand towards the graveyard, “These people are also important to me. I thought I’d share them with you- maybe let you see through to a part of me that no one else sees.”

    “I, erm…” In truth, Lena had difficulty digesting such a confession. Who was this person standing in front of her now compared to the tight-lipped, closed-off individual who once sat on the other side of a wide set of bars? “Kara, I’m honored.”

    “You remind me so much of my best friend. He had the same thirst for knowledge as you do, always hunting around for an answer, always putting things back together…”

    Lena veered her eyes sharply towards the smattering of graves again, and Kara pointed to one in particular. A slab of stone sat there over a stringy bed of straggler daisies. The name “Winslow Schott Jr.” was etched onto its front. His life —gathered between two different dates— lay just beneath.

    Born 2081.

    Died 2099.

    “Only eighteen?” Lena felt the sympathetic draw of her brow as she looked back towards Kara, astonished. “He was so young.”

    “Oh, Winn managed a lot in those eighteen years, though.” Kara said with a sad twinge to her voice, “He wasn’t made remnant like the rest of us.”

    “No?”

    Kara huffed a laugh, “He missed school that day. Stayed home sick.”

    “But you remained friends, I assume?”

    “We did.”

    “Good. I’m glad.”

    “I used to come here a lot,” Kara shrugged, “Just to talk, and to ask for advice. A lot of remnants are buried here. Many of them were my friends.”

    “You’ve lost so much, Kara. I’m so sorry.”

    “I mean, the end of the war was hard on everyone. It became an everyday struggle just to keep ourselves hidden and out of danger. Once the more physical attributes of our condition spread, and metahuman children were being born with scaled skin and things like that, the hatred was impossible to overcome.” Kara gestured towards the tombstones again, “This is what came of that hatred.”

    “Kara…”

    “Winn,” Kara hung her head, “…he died afraid, and in pain. He wouldn’t leave me, and it cost him his life.”

    “But he wasn’t afraid of you.”

    “No. That’s what helped me to realize just how alike you are. You both have this inherent goodness about you, and I—” Kara scratched nervously at her chin all of a sudden, “I want to tell you something.”

    Lena paused for a moment and then began wringing her hands together tightly. “What?”

    “Remember back when I told you the reason why I needed to escape my cell so badly? That it was the only thing left in the world that was worth anything at all?”

    “You were talking about your sister. I know that now.”

    “Well,” Kara dug her bootheel around in the dirt, “Turns out I was wrong.”

    “What are you talking about?”

    “I’m talking about you.” Kara confessed, reaching out to cup the underside of Lena’s chin just to hold her gaze, “In the grandest sense, you’re worth all of it. Every day, every hour.” She gave a sad smile, “You’re my reason now… My reason for living.”

    Lena couldn’t tear her eyes away from Kara. She felt frozen in time there, as if she were as fixed to it as Kara was. It didn’t take long for the words to come leaping out from her mouth…

    “Kara, I—”

    “I—”

    Both of them chuckled quietly amidst the small space of awkwardness between them, having spoken in unison with —what Lena could only hope— was the very same sentiment.

    “Go ahead.” Lena bowed her head politely.

    Kara took her by the hand and kissed over it several times, eventually hugging it to her chest like it were the dearest thing left on earth. “I do love you. I just wanted to say it plainly for once, just so you understand how much I mean it.”

    “I know how much.” Lena replied as she reached for the back of Kara’s head, asking her to rest it against her neck where she could feel each of her breaths. Kara obliged her this affection, just as she had for the past several days, now.

    Standing there inside the cemetery of Kara’s past helped to put into perspective the true value of the life she had lived, versus the life she lived now. Built upon so many bones, it was. So many innocent lives… Lost, but not forgotten. Kara pulled in one deep breath at a time and buried her face against the warmth of Lena’s neck. The thought of remaining like that forever crossed Lena’s mind, but it was always the forlorn reminder of her mortality to call her back towards reality. Kara had suffered so much already, but was still willing to suffer again once Lena eventually died? The love she had professed would never come into question for this reason alone. Lena knew that Kara would love her until the very end of her mortal life.

    Taking in more of her surroundings, Lena saw so many ribbons and dried, desiccated flowers laying around the rest of the graves. She looked a little closer at them all, and at the names that were mournfully carved into each stoneface.

    Laurel Lance.

    Oliver Queen.

    Nia Nal.

    Iris West.

    Kelly Olsen.

    Spanning centuries right down to the most recent decade, each grave seemed to tell a story. It was in the way that the lettering was shaped as it was carved into the stone— almost like someone had written there with the tip of their finger. Lena stepped back and palmed gently at the side of Kara’s face, turning her attention back to the cemetery.

    “Kara, did you…?”

    “Bury them?” Kara nodded with her reply, “Yeah. Most of them, anyway. Laurel over there, she was the last.” She shrugged uncomfortably then as if it pained her to remember, “Sara’s sister.”

    Lena’s face fought hard against the urge to burst into tears, but instead of breaking her resolve, she pulled a calm breath through her nose and cleared her throat. “I… don’t know what to say.”

    “You don’t have to say anything. Come on,” Kara tugged at Lena’s hand again, “Let me tell you a little more about my mom and dad.”

    “Alright. Lead the way.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Weird headcanon of mine that J'onn J'onzz has a wonky shaped heart. Not a Nyssa hatefic. Life starting to get back to normal now that I dropped the meds which were affecting me so severely. Glad to be moving on. Hopefully I'll be able to start editing more chapters of this story soon, several more written already, just waiting a second and third look. Still healing in the meantime.

    Thanks for reading. <3

    Chapter 18: The Wind in Your Hair, the Dust on My Skin

    Chapter Notes

    Slow morning, have another.

    Chapter Eighteen: The Wind in Your Hair, the Dust on My Skin

     

    “I still don’t like it.”

    “But my moms said it was okay…”

    “I know—” Kara pinched her lips together tightly in an effort to pursue a more diplomatic route, “It’s just that you don’t truly know this person like we do.”

    “That’s not a reason.” Natina insisted, jumping for the hatch door button. Kara engaged it with the side of her fist and then folded her arms in lieu of her goddaughter’s age-appropriate naivety. “She wants to help me.” The pigtailed fourteen-year-old said, “I don’t see what the big deal is.”

    Kara knew damn well that the only reason Sara agreed to this sour little arrangement was to keep the peace. Once Nat got it into her head that she wanted to study archery, it was only a matter of time before her sights landed on Nyssa, whose skill with a bow of any kind was unmatched far and wide.

    Perfect.

    Just perfect.

    The large doors groaned upon their tired tracks as they split apart slowly. There was still time to convince Nat to study medicine, or engineering for crying out loud… Wasn’t there? Maybe Kara could entice her with a ride out to the apple orchard. The bike was sitting right there inside the emergency hatch, nestled beneath the long turbine blades of their battle helicopter. Just waiting. Kara could still remember the day Nat had been stung by a bee for the first time. It was in that very same apple orchard, and against Kara’s instructions to remain completely still until the bee took its leave of her arm, Nat swatted at it, terrified, and it stung her for her trouble. Kara had felt as if the world had stopped spinning for those panic-stricken moments of crying that had come next. That was when it hit her; The realization that Natina Lance-Sharpe would do whatever she wanted despite her mothers’ warnings, or advice, or even their direct orders. It was simply Kara’s job to make sure that the child had a safe place to land whenever she would inevitably fall. She was headstrong like Sara that way, but wise beyond her years like Ava.

    There would be no stopping her.

    Kara knew it.

    Nat was eager to pass through the massive doors at first, but Kara caught her by the shoulder and stepped in front, more than willing to block a bullet should there be anyone lying in wait on the other side. Even though the Infinite’s location was deeply rooted in Union territory, one could never be too careful. Nat never complained. Her abundance of patience for most things proved to be one of her best qualities, but only out of necessity’s sake. She was nearly fifteen now and still showed no signs of her metahuman abilities. Rare was it that a meta’s powers lay dormant for the whole of their life— but still, Kara worried. In a world such as this, Nat would need any help she could get in order to survive on her own.

    Hence Nat’s insisting upon archery lessons.

    Satisfied that the coast was clear, Kara gave a toss of her head as an indication for Nat to follow. The fact that Nyssa chose training grounds outside of the Infinite’s earthen walls was doubly irksome. Their arrows could fly freely aboveground, yes, but what if it was all a ploy to abscond into the wilds with Nat in tow? Yet another insane attempt to pry Sara away from where her heart truly lied. Kara shook her head as they continued climbing the rocky slope towards higher ground and then gave a small glance over her shoulder back towards the hatch. They wouldn’t be able to get far should Nyssa seize the opportunity, not with the Apache chopper and their small fleet of Dahtiye flyers after them.

    Nat shrugged around the string of her bow and then shouldered Kara affectionately as they walked, presumably to alleviate the tension Kara held in her gait, “I mean, what’s the worst that could happen?”

    Kara’s mind reeled with Nyssa’s past attempts to win back the woman she loved; and the monster that she had created.

    “I gave you everything! A life worth living- but with me!”

    “You gave me death.”

    “Sara!”

    “No, Nyssa. I’m tired of the killing.”

    “…Kara?”

    Kara blinked rapidly, pulling herself back to the present. She only wished that she could tell Nat about the years-long kidnapping efforts, and the failed assassinations to take her other mother from her. “Yeah,” Kara cleared her throat, “Sorry, bud. I’m just overthinking it, that’s all. If your moms agreed to it, then I’ll back off.”

    “She’s… not really that bad, is she?”

    A low-pressure -pop- came forth from the empty air in front of them, and from the wispy, ethereal tendrils of a slowly dissipating black cloud emerged a voice.

    “Worse, I’m afraid.” Nyssa grinned, delighted in her mischief.

    Nat couldn’t help but to smile. It did something to the pit of Kara’s stomach, like a tightly furled fist seated in anger.

    “No games, Nyssa. Just the basics.” Kara seethed quietly while Nat placed her things down upon a nearby stump. There were targets erected not too far off from there; reeds gathered and bound from the sparse oases that dotted the surrounding rugged terrain. Kara noticed that they weren’t in their usual shape either, carefully stacked or barreled to take arrows in their middles… No, they were shaped like people. “You’ve gotta be kidding me—” Kara exasperated, “What, you’re gonna have her aim for the heart?”

    Nyssa’s expression reverted back to its usual, infuriatingly confident stare. “I will teach what is necessary.”

    “I heard about what happened between you and Lena the other day.” Kara took another step, “Had it been me who put a stop to it, then you wouldn’t be walking right now, let alone be able to hold a bow straight.”

    “Seriously?” Nat huffed on her way back, “We just got here and you already want to tear each other apart.”

    “Your collective of parents do seem very quick to anger, Natina.”

    “Hah—!” Kara hacked a laugh, “You’re a fine one to talk!”

    Nat growled beneath her breath and then stamped her small bootheel into the dirt, “Alright, quit it! Would you just go?!?”

    Kara was so taken aback that she nearly stumbled. “W-what?”

    “I—” Nat recovered quickly, and after a moment of wordless jaw-flapping, she threw her arms around Kara’s waist and hugged, “I mean, can you go now?”

    “You want me to leave?”

    “Yeah.”

    “…I don’t think that’s such a good idea, Nat.”

    “Please?”

    Kara looked back up at Nyssa, who had thankfully chosen to stay silent on the subject. She was busily cleaning beneath her fingernails with the sharp tip of her dagger and pretending as though she couldn’t hear. Nat squeezed around Kara’s middle again and then lowered her voice to a more affectionate tone, “I don’t want you to watch.”

    “Why not?”

    “…I don’t know. Just because?”

    Kara sighed heavily and —much to her chagrin— decided to acquiesce her goddaughter’s request. “I’ll be back in one hour.”

    “Three.” Nyssa spoke up again, though not so much as deigning to lift her gaze. Kara had to fight the hard clench of her teeth just at the sound of her voice again. She was still so wounded by the other woman’s unsubtle disregard for life, namely Alex’s life. Her vote towards the effort to rescue Kara’s sister was still a firm “no”. Kara squared her shoulders defiantly and asserted herself a second time…

    “Two.”

    Seeing Nyssa’s mouth fall open with a slight gape, Nat chimed in before either of them could protest further. “Two! Two, Okay?”

    Kara gave one last hard look towards Nyssa, who was eye-to-eye with her again and seemingly agreeable to the terms which had been laid out. Or so she was forced to assume as much, that level stare of Nyssa’s was difficult to interpret at times. Leaving Nat there with a woman who was likely the world’s most skilled assassin was hard enough, but Kara also knew that it would be virtually impossible to keep a sharp eye on them both, even from a distance. Nyssa had an eerie sixth sense for being watched; one of the many things which made her so incredibly dangerous. Her callous and almost cruel nature was just the cherry on top.

    Despairingly, Kara chose to take the long way home, but stopped halfway and veered east, missing the Infinite’s entrance entirely. She would go and sit with her dearly departed again, where she could speak her mind and lay her brow upon their headstones, asking for guidance. So much time she had spent there already…

    Admittedly, it was hard to take herself away from them anymore.

     

     

    The sight of Lena’s beautifully dimpled smile made it all go away.

    The ponytail of her impossibly straight raven-colored hair fell over her shoulder as she cleared the ER’s examination bench. There were blood-stained rags and various tools there which had been used to aid in the removal of an ingrown scale— a procedure which Lena had regaled Kara with almost as soon as she had arrived that afternoon. The fact that she was still so unshakable by the more pronounced visages of some of the Infinite’s metas only lent a hand to her ability to make friends with them. Lena’s fearlessness was a hugely welcome constant in Kara’s life. After all, she had so few of those after so many years…

    “And Sara was okay with that?” Lena asked, still interested in Natina’s endeavor to learn archery.

    “Mm,” Kara grimaced, “Not really, no.”

    “Oh?”

    “She kinda took my head off once she figured out that I had left them alone together.”

    Lena nodded as she drew a freshly bleached sheet over the bench, “Well, I’m pleased to see that you’re still in one piece.”

    “Besides, Sara’s not actually the one I should be afraid of…”

    “What do you mean?” Lena turned around with a guess, “Ava?”

    The row of Kara’s teeth clearly displayed in an embarrassed manner was all the answer she could give. Lena had quickly learned of the unspoken faux pas that was asking for the specifics of a meta’s abilities. Such was a privilege that came with time, of course, but the doctor that was bound within her made it difficult at first to realize— it was rude to ask someone whether they could fly or see through walls before asking for their name.

    The dark cloud that followed Kara wherever she went came rolling in overhead again, and she hung her head where the space of silence stood between them. It came in waves, still. Mostly when the days drew to an end, and there was nothing left to be done. Kara’s mind would reel back to her sister, and she would become depressed again. Lena was fast to react, like always.

    “Hey…” Lena went to stand face-to-face with Kara, pawing over her sweetly and frowning at her rapid decline, “Everything turned out alright, didn’t it?”

    It was all Kara could do just to tuck her chin with a nod.

    “Come here, my darling.” Lena reached for the back of Kara’s neck.

    Kara sighed for the hundredth time that day, “I just don’t know what I’m doing anymore.”

    “Yes, you do.”

    “No,” Kara dropped her brow carefully atop the slender perch of Lena’s shoulder, “I really don’t think I do. I feel lost- but only when you’re not in the room.”

    “Baby…”

    “I just want—” Kara stopped at the sound of a frustrated grunt coming from outside. It was “Mick” shaped, bellied and impatient like always. She looked up and reached for the long, shuttered slats of the window behind Lena and lifted them open. There stood the bald-headed schoolteacher, pacing a short line between the ferns and grumbling his discontent for the small, white flowers in his hands. They seemed to droop too much for his liking. Kara knew that he often traveled a long way for them, and that he was obviously disappointed with their sad-looking state. “Hang on a sec.” Kara said.

    “What is it?”

    “A very, very long story.” Kara replied as she stepped away to round the corner into the next room, “I’ll be right back.”

    Caitlin was tapping away at the holoscreen in front of her, a mix of dials and iterations of the same mathematic solution. Kara knocked at the broad side of a steel cabinet to get her attention…

    “Flower delivery.”

    “Huh?” Caitlin asked, swiveling around in her chair.

    “The snow drop kind.”

    “…Ah.”

    It didn’t take long for Caitlin’s hands to abandon the holoscreen, electing to run them each through her long, coppery locks instead as a means to tidy herself up. She almost went as far as to unfasten the top button of her teal blouse, but decided against it as soon as she noticed that Kara’s eyes were still upon her.

    She huffed, annoyed, “Oh, shut up.”

    Kara held her hands up quickly in defense, “I didn’t say anything.”

    The unbelieving roll of Caitlin’s eyes barely masked her excitement as she exited the ER. She would be hard-pressed to admit it, but she was smitten with the burly caretaker. He was soft only for her, and that seemed to melt her ice-laden heart like a charm. Mick burned brightly for Caitlin. Everyone could see it. Kara took satisfaction with this; it was at least one thing that seemed to be going right. Apart from herself and Lena, of course.

    When Kara stepped back into the exam room, Lena had let her hair down and was leaned against the table with her arms folded, waiting. Coat abandoned, collar undone. Just like that, all of the stress and woe and worry that Kara held in her shoulders fell away. A heaved breath of relief went out from her nose, and before either of them knew it, their reaching hands gave way to impassioned kisses, and then more small, blissful sighs of their reuniting. They were magnetized to one another in this way, sharing almost every moment alone as such since their first desperate act of falling together.

    “You’re the light of my life…” Kara whispered, drawing her lips down from the reddened lobe of Lena’s ear. “It’s like I can’t see anything without you by my side.”

    Lena shivered inside of Kara’s arms and mewled quietly in her euphoric state, “Then give me your darkness- I want all of you, Kara.”

     

     

    The trembling beset in every glorious inch of Kara’s muscle was akin to the deep rumbling of earth. The terrible quaking of large, tectonic powers, and the crumbling of mountainous peaks that came along with it.

    Lena threw herself into the very middle of it; That disastrous void which sought to swallow her up completely. She would ride it with proud gusts of wind through her hair, haunting every last gale with the sound of her voice…

    “Fuck—” She keened with her head thrown back. The hand that gripped at her waist pulled hard to right her on top of the other, thrust firmly between her legs.

    Unlike the first time where she had ridden Kara’s long fingers to her utter ruin, the small pinch of pain was gone and her hips were pleased to rotate on their very own, steadily onward like the evenly distributed steel of train wheels. The water of their hidden oasis rippled with each drop of her waist into Kara’s lap—

    “Hm,” She hummed, “Mm- I love you, Kara.”

    Without fail, those were the words to send Kara’s tremors into their full-blown state. The sweeping wind licked at Lena’s backside once she was up and out of the water, chilling the wet surface of her skin all the way up the backs of her thighs. The smooth boulder that met her back was even colder— it sent a sharp gasp of surprise raking through the thin space of Lena’s teeth, but only to be outweighed by the moan that came from behind it. Kara poured herself over Lena like the long claw of shade from a hillside. Strong and steady.

    “God, you feel so good…” Kara said, moaning intermittently into Lena’s mouth.

    Content at first just to take a dip into the cool waters several miles from the Infinite’s grand shelter, Kara was unable to resist the smooth touch of Lena’s skin. Lena didn’t stop her.     “Now, stay very still.”

    Kara nodded wordlessly. Her hands went to latch onto the curved arch above her, needing something to hold. Her first thought went to the fabric binder around her chest —and that Lena might rid her of it altogether— but Lena skipped it completely, choosing instead to peck one small kiss after another below it on her way down. She paused over every protrusion of Kara’s abdominal muscles with her lips pressed to them, worshipping each taut line and the warmth that emanated there. For once, time was on her side. It felt like years.

    Lena was on her knees and undoing the belt holding Kara’s pants up. They fell to the floor with a -fwump- and -clack-, and a stiff jolt ran throughout Kara’s arms as soon as Lena’s tongue made its first loving swipe. Bits of centuries-hardened clay fell from the arch where Kara’s fingers dug in, desperate to grab at something, anything, to keep herself still just as Lena had said.

    “God—”

    Lena’s hands grabbed at the backs of Kara’s thighs then, urging her forward onto her mouth to take as much of her as she could. The wet sort of sounds all of it made…

    Oh, my God.

    Oh, my God, oh my God, oh my God…

    The glossy brown undertone of Lena’s hair bobbed back and forth, and Kara felt that her eyes were unable to look anywhere else. It was bewitching. She was completely under her spell. No one had ever done something quite like this before. Kara was always the one to take charge, not the other way around…

    And she was thrilled.

     

     

    “See, I wasn’t lying.”

    Lena eventually shooed away two frustrated lab coats from the guarded exam room, ushering them out and into the hall. She didn’t like the way their hands impatiently grabbed at Kara’s arms. They had tried everywhere in their effort to locate a vein willing enough to cooperate. The backs of her hands, the bends of her arms, even her neck— which, when the needle was jabbed, had broke in half just as the rest had.

    Not often was Lena left alone with Kara, but she trusted that the ire of Kara’s circumstances had passed by now. Lena no longer walked down the long corridor to the tune of her newest guest’s enraged screaming. It was almost as if Kara were trying to please her, lately. She fought only the way the sentries took her from one place to another, earning her several shocks from her collar each day. Lena was intrigued to find a lack of burn marks there at Kara’s neck, being able to see it so closely now.

    “You don’t want someone in here with us?” Kara asked. Her eyes seemed to be traveling all across the features of Lena’s face, as if they couldn’t decide where to land first.

    Lena turned the corners of her mouth down with an agreeable frown and shook her head, “I don’t believe you to be reckless enough to try and harm me.”

    “Don’t be so sure.”

    “I’ve actually yet to see you do anything violent enough to warrant that kind of fear. I’m not saying you’re not capable of it,” Lena gestured for Kara’s arm again, who gave it freely and without complaint, “I’m just saying that you’ve been choosing not to. Where my staff is concerned, anyway. I know that you could throw them straight through some of these walls if you wanted to. So, why not?”

    “I dunno.” Kara replied, shrugging her ear to her shoulder, “That stuff I was gassed with makes me sleepy still.”

    “No, it doesn’t.”

    Kara yanked her arm back out of spite, and Lena startled. They paused, staring at one another for a small moment, and then Kara returned to her previous position. Progress… Lena thought. She went back to inspecting Kara’s arm from shoulder to fingertip again.

    “Anyway,” Kara broke their silence, “I told you this wouldn’t work. I haven’t seen my own blood since I was a kid. Why can’t you just take what you need from my spit, or something?”

    Lena was running her thumbs over the faint, bluish color of Kara’s veins inside the bend of her arm, amazed for the fact that they were indeed in there and right beneath the surface, but no matter what they tried, they simply couldn’t reach them. The tensile strength and hardness of Kara’s skin was comparable to diamonds, yet still soft to the touch. So much so that Lena was lost in the way that it felt in her hands. Kara’s body was incredible. A medical marvel.

    “Doc?”

    “Hm- yes?” Lena blinked, “Oh, erm- well… In practice, saliva doesn’t yield as much DNA as a blood sample does. I should like to study as much of your genetic coding as possible, and taking your blood is the best way to analyze the specific strains of the code that I’m after. The only way, really.”

    “Hate to break it to you, gorgeous, but it’s not gonna happen.”

    Lena sighed, smoothing her hand over the inside of Kara’s elbow one last time and then relinquishing it back to her. “Very well,” She said, “I’ll just have to get what I need from you in a different way.”

    “Like how?”

    “I’m not sure. We could… talk?”

    The sound of a sneeze jostled Lena awake gently, and she peeled open the heavy lids of her eyes to see Kara sitting up in bed, swatting a small floating down feather away from her nose. Lena smiled and rocked herself up onto her elbow, yawning sleepily around her concern. “What’s wrong?”

    “We, um,” Kara looked over her shoulder back towards her empty space on the bed, “…I guess we knocked enough of the stuffing loose last night. I’ll have to get more from one of the crane walks whenever we get back.” She drew her eyes then to the rucksack hanging on a hook along the opposite wall, “If we get back, I mean.”

    Lena slunk her shoulder back inside the large shirt she slept in and crawled across the bed to straddle and sit in Kara’s lap. She laid her head there upon Kara’s broad shoulder and hummed at the warm feeling of her skin pressed against her cheek. “We’ll be back. Sooner than you think.”

    “I keep thinking about Nat, and whether or not I should say goodbye.”

    “You’ll do what’s best for her either way, I know it.”

    “She’ll just be upset that I’ve gone again…”

    Lena leaned back again to look Kara in her remarkably sad eyes, “Kara, do you want to stay?”

    “No,” Kara shook her head adamantly, “No. Where you go, I go.”

    Settled once more, Lena placed her head back down. “Don’t worry. As long as we have each other, everything will be alright.”

    “The sun’s gonna be up soon.” Kara said with an almost reluctant sigh.

    “Okay. Well… Will you hold me until then?”

    Kara nodded affectionately against Lena’s neck and wrapped both of her arms around her waist, “Of course. Whatever you want.”

     

    —End of Act II—

    Chapter 20: Wherefore Art Thou, My God?
