    “Why are you doing this?” She sobbed.

    “For the very same reason I have always said.” He replied. Lena flinched as he reached out to thumb at the wet tracks along her cheeks, “It’s just you and I, my dear. We’re remaking the world in our image.”

    Lena tried adjusting her shoulders beneath the wide strap that held them down. It was so pulled so tight that she struggled to breathe. “What have you done with my friends?”

    “Friends! Hah!”

    “What have you done with them?!” She shouted.

    “Don’t you see that they’re of no concern to you?” Her father shouted back angrily, “What does a lion care for lambs?!”

    “I’m not a lion!”

    “Yes, you are!”

    “Daddy—”

    “First ‘monster’, now ‘daddy’…” He made several -tsks- as he backed away. “Why is it always ‘daddy’ whenever you want something?” Lionel then stepped over to a wide instrument table with another long sigh and began shuffling things around there, “I can forgive your betrayals simply because you are my daughter, and it is because you are my daughter that I will bestow upon you the most ultimate of gifts. I know how strong you are,” Lionel hung his head in shame for a moment, “Unlike your brother.”

    “My… brother?” Lena uttered breathily, ceasing her struggle to free herself.

    Lionel turned around again with a syringe now in his hand. He waved it around as he spoke with a terrifying sense of grandeur, “Aside from ‘savior’, there is one other title that I value above all else. Being a father to so many need only disappointment me so much until one was able to stand apart from the rest. You are my legacy, Lena. I wouldn’t trouble you with the pain I’m about to inflict if I didn’t think you were capable of overcoming it.”

    “Please…” Her eyes went towards the large syringe with its almost viridian shade of green inside, “…don’t.”

    “For decades I went in search of Subject Zero.”

    All of a sudden, the recollection of her eye-opening conversation with J’onn crashed to the forefront of her mind.

    “I thought him dead, destroyed by the vaccine which he had created.”

    Lena sobbed louder, “Don’t do this!”

    “I will say, though, how fortuitous it was that you brought the martian right to our doorstep. That, I must thank you for. With its help, I’ve finally synthesized a more stable method of delivery. No more bone marrow transplants to keep me alive and able-bodied. This will make you powerful beyond your wildest dreams, dear daughter.”

    “No—!”

    Lionel gripped her by the jaw and craned her head forcefully to the left. A line of white coats entered the room and held her still, ignoring her screams for help.

    “You won’t have to worry about your friends once you’ve healed.” He whispered in her ear before plunging the needle deep into her neck, “You’ll forget about them after a century or so. I promise.”

     

     

    “Mm…”

    The gentle caress of birdsong fell upon her ears, stirring her spirit with a pleasantness. Lena hummed once more with the warmth of the sun on her face. She loved it here. Wherever “here” was, exactly… A cool breeze swept through the length of her silken hair, and she smiled with her eyes closed.

    “Are you happy?”

    Kara’s voice. It felt like a memory. But, was it?

    “Mhm.” Lena nodded.

    She felt her cheek land against the notch between Kara’s collarbones; her favorite place of refuge, still damp from her tears.

    “I wish we could stay like this forever.”

    Kara beckoned Lena’s eyes open with a gentle kiss to her forehead. “That’s what we are.”

    Confused, Lena peeked away from the warm space of Kara’s neck to slowly look behind them. There were her mountains, just as her mother had promised. Lena puzzled her brow to remember exactly— where had her mother gone? And why couldn’t she recall the shape of her face?

    “What do you mean?” Lena asked.

    Kara hugged her closer just as a listless group of clouds was suddenly swept away; the hand of Kara’s God making clear Lena’s vision.

    “We’re forever.” Kara whispered.

    Lena suddenly gasped as the bent and twisted limbs of all the mountain’s trees quickly came into focus. It filled her with terror. She realized there weren’t any trees at all, nor were there any mountains.

    There were only bodies. Piled high on top of one another. Thousands, and thousands—

    Lena lurched forward and clutched at her stomach, ill for what she had seen inside a mere memory— or was it a dream? She couldn’t be sure. How was it that they felt so alike?

    Beads of sweat had collected upon her brow and she went to swipe there with the back of her hand, but the movement of her arm trailed longways in front of her face. A hallucination of some sort? Lena continued staring at her hand, amazed for how it undulated to and fro as if beneath water. She was glad to not be tied down anymore… but when had she been moved to a bed? The “where” and the “why” gradually became more and more meaningless. She was tired again all of a sudden. Too tired to inspect the sheets that were wrapped so tightly around her legs as if she had writhed inside of them for many hours. With a slow slump forward, Lena mewled one final plea with the remainder of her quickly dissipating consciousness.

    But at least the grass was soft.

    She combed through its blades with her fingertips to right herself with the world again. There was a feeling of relief filling up the space in her chest left by her sense of dread. Lena took a deep breath in and smelled the familiar scent of home tenfold. Pine. Musk. A crisp morning dew.

    She yawned at last and blinked her eyes open to greet the sturdy face of a gravestone. Gone was the peacefulness that rested deep in her bones. Lena sat up, horrified.

    The slab in front of her was crooked, seemingly sunken into the earth over an innumerable measure of years. The words inscribed there read “Ruby Arias” with a set of dates beneath, which were all but eroded from the stone itself. A row of similar headstones appeared right alongside…

    “Samantha Arias”

    “Natina Lance-Sharpe”

    “Caitlin Snow”

    “Barry Allen”

    Lena shook her head furiously. She refused to believe what she saw. This had to be another hallucination, but how would she find her way out? “Think of something else…” She told herself as she shut her eyes to the world, “Anything else. Think.”

    A flock of scampering meta children chasing after their caretaker.

    Freshly baked snickerdoodles.

    A glowing sea.

    The grandest of trees.

    Books. Walls and walls of books.

    Lena opened her eyes to find herself now the dead-center of a gravestone circle. They closed in on her from all around, scoring long drag marks into the earth as they went…

    “No! No, I don’t want to be here—” She looked up towards a bright blue sky and shouted, “I don’t want to see this! Take me back!” Lena cried, “I want to go back!”

    There was only the feeling of hands adjusting her back into bed, and the careful brushing of her hair from her face. Still too weak to wake herself fully, Lena did what she could to bend away from it all again. To make herself small again. Innocent again.

    -slap-

    “I warned you! You should have given me what you promised!”

    Lena knew the sound of her father’s yelling more readily than most others. It was all he seemed to do, lately. It grieved her to hear him so upset; He had always been so loving. Until she had grown older, that is… and apparently, less remarkable than he had hoped. She only wished to be worthy in his eyes. Was that not why he pushed her so hard? To learn, and to be remarkable?

    “I’m sorry—”

    “You are nothing to me, now.”

    “Please, my love.”

    Her father’s yelling, but also her mother’s crying…

    Lena unfurled herself from where she hid beneath a table and looked across a polished wood floor just as her mother was struck again. She landed like a doll flopped onto their arms, much like Lena had done with her own— haphazardly tossing them upon the floor without another thought. It broke her heart. Her mother was not a doll.

    The lids of her mother’s eyes were still open with shock, but there seemed to be nothing in the way of light behind them anymore. The color red streamed downward from her temple and began to pool upon the floor near one of the table’s legs, and Lena began to cry.

    It was the -tap- of her father’s cane that made her jolt with fear.

    He leaned down to offer a hand beneath the tabletop and ushered her with a sweeping motion to come out. His hair was gray, and his face was colored with disappointment…

    “With me, my dear. Come along. We shall find some other use for you.”

    Lena woke again with the memory of her begging him to stay; to never come out from under that table. He was less gentle with her from then on, not caring so much as to even look her in the eyes on most days. She had hidden from him for weeks afterward. Somehow, Lena had forgotten about the incident altogether as her father had pushed her daily fencing lessons upon her. Her studies. Her routines.

    How could she forget?

    Finally coherent enough to sit up from the narrow cot of a familiar observation room, Lena looked around, blinking slowly. She knew exactly where she was. It was the fact that she was now on the other side of it all which unsettled her greatly. She knew these cells to be far on the other side of the compound. The same ones where she had interviewed previous residents— whom she knew now to be stolen metahumans. Lena’s head fell into her hands as she began to weep. Not for herself, or for her situation, but for her mother whose name she was still yet to recall.

    All this time, her face had been hidden deep within Lena’s subconscious. But more so, the shape it had kept whenever she had been killed…

    Murdered by Lena’s father.

     

     

    “Lena—” Kara bolted upright and fought to catch her breath.

    The back of her head ached badly, which she knew was probably the reason why she had trouble piecing things together at first. It was still dark, but Kara had the sneaking suspicion that she was no longer outside. The smell of fresh air was absent. In its place was the acrid, dingy scent of basement flooring, where bacteria flourished from leaky pipes and a lack of sterilizing sunlight.

    “Great.” A voice said from somewhere amidst the dark. There was the tone of a branded sarcasm in it. It was one that Kara knew well. “I told them you were dead, but they insisted you weren’t. Thanks for getting me stuck down here with you, by the way.”

    “Down—” Kara stopped.

    …Here?

    Her head spun around. She reached out into the darkness to place a hand flat against the wall.

    Right where she knew it would be.

    Not here.

    Kara shot to her feet and felt along at her arms. Soft. Clean. A gray sweatsuit, no doubt. Her mind began to race.

    Not again…

    “No—” She expelled the word like a breathy curse and darted towards the only available light just beyond a familiar row of bars. Lena’s chair and end table were no longer sat just on the other side. “No!” Kara shouted.

    “Don’t try anything stupid.”

    “Lena!”

    “Hey—”

    “Lena!”

    The swirling tempest of fears between Kara’s ears had narrowed into a single file string of imperatives. Get out. Get Lena. Become whole again. The sister she had once believed dead was now within arm’s reach, yet it suddenly meant little to nothing. Alex had been thrown inside with her. A companion for her own personal hell.

    Kara gripped two of the bars and began pulling them apart with all of her might.

    “Hey!” Alex shuffled to her feet behind her, a tone of warning replacing that irascible sarcasm, “You’re gonna—”

    “I have to get out of here.”

    “You’re not listening. Stop—”

    “No, I have to get Lena!”

    “I said stop, you crazy person! You’ll set off the—”

    Right on cue, the tell-tale -pop- of ultraviolet light and searing screech of a siren brought Kara to her knees. Her hands clapped automatically over her ears, and she rolled forward onto her forehead, trying her best to drown out the alarm with the internalized sounds of her pained groaning. Even though it was almost too painful to open her eyes even in the slightest bit, Kara managed to witness her sister lying there on the stark white floor beside her, curled into a fetal position and screaming through the hard clench of her teeth.

    The reminder of it all was awful. A fate worse than death.

    The alarm —having become more than acquainted with it during her first stay— seemed almost brand new, for some reason, as if it were all happening again for the very first time. The jarring, ear-splitting nature of it was likely something she’d never get used to. That much was clear. At least this time, it cut off after only several seconds or so. Enough to get them both down on the ground and disoriented, she realized.

    Kara peeled her eyes open again to the sound of the heavy door on the other side of the bars— but nobody stepped inside. An object came flying through instead, which landed with a tinny -clank- and began spewing thick, green fog.

    That, Kara would never forget.

    “Now look what you did!”

    “Dammit…” Kara backpedaled on her hands and hips just as Alex did.

    They could only escape the gas up until it reached the last corner of the room, with both of them vying for space to tuck themselves inside as quickly as they could. Kara decided just to concede and throw herself out of the running for the last bit of good air. She coughed through the stinging wetness of her eyes and watched as Alex, now taken up completely by the gas, lumped forward unconsciously. Kara fought like hell just to keep her head upright; she would be damned if this were the last time she would ever see her sister again. Her vision began to strobe as pairs of mission-oriented boots filtered into the room. Sterile suits, masks and all. They took Alex and dragged her along the floor towards the exit by the heels of her socked feet, and Kara raised a hand to plead with them…

    “Wait—” She begged as she chugged one last ragged cough. Each passing second went along with the loud -thump- of her heart, and finally, she felt that her head was simply too heavy.

    It came down with a -crack- against the tile, echoing the last remaining threads of her consciousness.

    Kara couldn’t believe it.

    This has to be a dream…

    Please, God, let this be a dream.

    Chapter End Notes

    Chapter title is, you guessed it, another Dickinson nod. Can't help myself. I felt that the earth-shattering realization that they both ended up in the place they began from deserved a whole chapter of its own. Kara's becoming more out of character with her sister in the picture now, making mistakes and fumbling her way towards resolutions. We should see Lena embrace the other side of that coin soon. Both of them will metamorphose for the better and, after coming full circle, they'll find their way back to one another. I promise.

    My updates are still sort of unpredictable, I'm sorry. Can't even begin to tell you when the next one will be fully edited and posted-- but its safe to say that you won't have to wait months. That would be too long. I can see the story coming to a close and being fully online by the end of spring. Just as a supposed deadline. Maybe before then. Hopefully the following chapters come as a good surprise to you, something to look forward to reading by the time you get home, or whatever the case.

    Like always, thanks for reading. <3

    Chapter 24: A Father’s Love, a God’s Forgiveness

    Chapter Notes

    TW: mentions of blood, non-graphic violence

    Chapter Twenty-Four: A Father’s Love, a God’s Forgiveness

     

    It was the timely -thud- of her socked feet against the hard floor. The round of Lena’s heel took hold of the tile and propelled her forward. Back and forth. Back and forth. She had read about predator cats in captivity from the world before. How cells of “environmental terrorists” had sought to free them by any means necessary, for which she concurred wholeheartedly. Especially now.

    Feeling like a caged animal forced quite the perspective, to be sure.

    Gone were her hours of banging her fist upon the large observation window. There was no such thing as privacy in her drab, gray environs. Sterile was the lot of it. Stranger yet, the dread-filled realization that she was now on the other side of things made her constantly sick. Lena’s quiet anxiety never used to make her so nauseous, but every time she so much as opened her mouth to speak, she felt the sudden urge to bow over the toilet. Shouting was out of the question. She managed a whisper the day before, but with no one around but the odd sentry bringing each of her breakfast and dinner trays, there wasn’t really any point in speaking at all. For all her absence of words, she more than made up for with tears.

    ...There came the wailing from down the hall again.

    Lena bolted upright from where she had sat on the floor and clapped her hands over her ears, inwardly begging for the sound to stop.

    It sounded so much like Sara.

     

     

    -whir-

    -clip-

    -clip-

    -clip-

    -clip-

    -whir-

    -clip-

    -clip-

    “Oh, my god, would you stop.”

    Kara huffed an unsurprised breath through her nose at the request. This “Smith” was more like her sister than she even realized, always asking questions in the form of demands. Kara was determined to make her remember, but first, she needed to get under her skin. Such was a sister’s duty.

    She twirled the spent canister of gas with her fingers again, making it skip over its clumsy dents and scrape against the unforgiving floor. Alex, or… Smith, as she kept calling herself, sighed for the hundredth time and gave an impatient pinch to the bridge of her nose. Kara smiled.  

    “I mean, there’s not much to do ‘round here except wait to die. Or, you know… be experimented on.” Kara said half-heartedly.

    Smith rolled her eyes. She only looked more and more like Alex whenever she did things like that.

    “Why don’t you just give them what they want, then?” She asked.

    Kara reared her head back, “You can’t seriously think that they’ll just let us go if I do? Besides, even if I could, I wouldn’t.”

    “Why not?”

    “Why not?!” Kara spat the question back, “Have you looked around lately? What the hell do you think is going on here?”

    Smith frowned and tucked her knees closer to her chest, “I try not to think about it.”

    “Hm.” Kara dropped her head back against the opposite wall, sounding a warning -beep- from the security system. “I think maybe you’re right.”

    “…Excuse me?”

    Kara closed her eyes. “Maybe you’re not my sister after all.”

    “Finally, she listens.”

    “My sister wasn’t such a goddamned coward.”

    There was the swift shifting of sweatsuit material from across the cell, and then the next thing Kara knew, the empty gas cylinder that she had been passing the time with came to land with a brittle -smack- against her collarbone. She opened her eyes and lunged for it angrily, intent on throwing it back. Smith dodged it as it crumpled on impact near the wall directly behind her head.

    “Hey!”

    “Coward.”

    “Shut up!”

    “Traitor.”

    “I said shut up!”

    Smith launched herself across the cell without another word, landing a blow straight across Kara’s chin. Kara would have been taken aback at the action if it didn’t smack of the exact opposite of her sister. It was for that reason alone that she rolled to her feet and grappled the waist in front of her. In this case, fighting a stranger was easy. Too easy.

    “Let go—”

    “No, fuck you!”

    “Argh- stop!”

    “Why don’t you just admit it! I wanna hear you say it!” Kara seethed as she tried pinning Smith to the floor.

    Smith eventually came down flat against her back and clawed at Kara’s face until she was completely overpowered. Her exhausted breaths buffeted some of her loose, auburn hair away from her eyes, which was where Kara could see the pain that lied within. Not hate, not rage… Just pain.

    “Alright!” Smith choked back a sob, “Ow- you’re hurting me!”

    “I don’t care.”

    “Let me go!”

    “No.”

    “Fine, I—” Smith turned her face away, ashamed, “I’m scared, okay? I’m always scared. Is that what you wanna hear?”

    Kara shook her head, “No, actually.”

    “Then what the hell do you want from me?!”

    “I want you to tell me what you’re really doing here.”

    “I just told you—”

    “The truth, dammit!”

    “That is the truth!” Smith shouted, her face red in its middle for all of her effort, “I’ve been hiding from the world ever since I can remember!”

    Kara loosened her grip slightly, “But, here…?”

    “Tell me a better place to hide from the enemy than right under their noses.”

    That was more Alex than Kara could stomach at the time, apparently. She shuffled on her hands and knees to get away quickly and leaned against the opposite wall as she caught her breath. It wasn’t the original sight of her, or hearing the sound of her voice for the first time in ten years, or even being trapped in an underground lab within arm’s reach of her that gave Kara pause… It was knowing beyond all rhyme or reason that Alex was still in there, having heard from the very depths of her soul like a desperate cry for help. Kara was convinced. She was still worth saving.

    “You really don’t remember me, do you?”

    Smith rubbed her wrist, wincing once or twice, “Answer’s not gonna get any different no matter how many times you ask me. I don’t know you.”

    “Maybe not now, but… you used to.”

    “Yeah, well, there were probably a lot of people I used to know that I don’t anymore.”

    “What do you mean?” Kara watched Smith bend her wrist back and forth a few more times, “Don’t worry," She tried offering some sort of consolation, "...remnants are hard to break.”

    “No shit, Sherlock.”

    “So, you do know that you’re a remnant…” Kara huffed, more as a statement of fact rather than a question.

    “Uh, kinda hard to miss whenever you’re walking around a desert with the other half of your brain in your hand. All I remember is limping around and holding my head together for days until some of the unmarred found me.”

    “Humans, you mean.”

    “Whatever.”

    “They… took care of you?”

    “Yeah, until these guys showed up.” Smith nodded upwards, gesturing towards the armed sentries who were no doubt milling around above.

    Kara shifted uncomfortably on her hips, “I wish I knew where to begin. There’s so much I want to apologize for...”

    “Don’t bother.”

    “If you would just let me—”

    “I said don’t bother. God, you’re persistent. I’ll give you that.”

    Kara grumbled beneath her breath and dropped her hands back into her lap, sliding down the wall to sit with a small -crash-. “So,” She quirked her chin, “What happened after that?”

    Smith only shook her head in reply.

    “They killed them, didn’t they? For being sympathizers?”

    “Not at first.” Smith sighed after a long pause, “They were after the metas there. I offered to fix one of their guns, and the next thing I knew, they were grabbing me and throwing me in a train car- telling me that there was more work to be done.”

    “They didn’t realize you were a remnant?”

    “No, and I tried keeping it that way until you and your friends showed up.”

    Kara folded her arms across her chest defiantly, “What were you gonna do in another ten years, huh? When everyone around you started showing signs of aging? Gray hair, lines on their faces… What were you gonna do then?”

    “I…” Smith shrugged and then scooted towards the empty cot, climbing up and onto it with another long sigh. It was difficult for her to move around without her knee brace, but she had managed well enough thus far. “I dunno. Start somewhere else, I guess. Somewhere new.”

    “As if they’d just let their best blacksmith go.”

    “I- I don’t wanna talk to you anymore. Just leave me alone.”

    Kara sat forward on her knees then, prostrating herself in front of the woman whom she still believed to be her sister, “Alex—”

    “That’s not my name!” The volume of Smith’s voice was enough to make Kara run stiff with a jolt.

    “Fine- Smith… Will you please just look at me, at least? Please?”

    Smith hesitated for a moment and then dragged her eyes back across the cell to witness Kara quite literally begging on her knees.

    “You really don’t know who I am?”

    Finally, and for the first time in days, Smith gazed upon Kara with a sense of openness, her intent clearly written all over her face. She was trying to remember. Kara’s heart sank whenever Smith shook her head apologetically.

    “No,” She replied, “…I’m sorry.”

     

     

    How long had it been?

    Lena pressed her forehead to her knees to stop the room from its continuous spinning. Everything about it was still so harsh upon her senses— the light, the sound, even the crisp feeling of her bedding beneath her fingertips.

    After so many days, she wouldn’t have been surprised if she had all but forgotten the sound of her own voice. She had practically given up on speaking. It wasn’t as if any of the sentries on this side of the compound were listening, anyway. They kept to themselves as they passed back and forth in front of the window to Lena’s room, not giving her so much as a passing glance. Lena would see them escorting different metahumans down the long hall every once in a while, walking them in one direction and then having to drag them back in the other. She could only imagine what they were doing to them, though, she didn’t have to imagine very hard. All of it used to be at her behest. The questions, the tests… Poetic justice seemed to serve her well here. She had so much time to think about it, after all.

    The shadow from a passing sentry caught her attention just as she looked up from the tops of her knees. They had someone with long, dark hair gripped tightly by their arm, and Lena gasped audibly at the sight of Ruby’s face once she was able to focus more clearly.

    Not Ruby, she realized.

    This person was older, taller, and much wearier, almost as if they had completely given up on life.

    Lena crawled from her narrow bed to the window and pawed her way along it as they passed by, smacking it with the flat of her hand to get their attention.

    “Samantha?” She coughed sharply against the dryness of her throat and tried again, “Samantha Arias?”

    Much to her surprise, the woman’s head spun around in her direction. Her eyes were wide, alert for the first time in what Lena could only assume was a long time.

    “Are you Samantha Arias?” Lena slowly tracked alongside them on her side of the glass.

    The woman nodded, and the sentry gave her a stiff yank at her arm for her trouble. She tried slowing her pace to speak with Lena, too invested now to take another step, “How do you know my name?”

    Lena smiled as her eyes filled with tears. Her throat felt as if it were on fire, “I know your daughter.”

    “Ruby?” The woman named Samantha said, her eyes growing more wild by the second, “Ruby- my baby? Is she safe?!”

    “Yes!”

    The sentry suddenly forced Samantha’s head down by the back of her neck and shouted at her to keep walking, which caused Lena to raise her own voice for the first time in days. “Stop that- don’t you touch her!” She started slamming the side of her fist against the glass again, anger seeping into every fiber of her being now, “Let her go!”

    Oddly enough, the large windowpane in front of her seemed to shudder in place just from the force of her breath upon it.

    Lena stopped, but before she could so much as gather up an explanation, the door to her observation room clicked open, and through it swept an armed sentry with the most serious of expressions tucked inside of their helmet.

    “Back away from the glass.” They said, raising the snubbed nose of their automatic weapon.

    “Listen to me, please,” Lena placed her hands out in front of herself, surrendering immediately, “I need you to take me to my father—”

    “Get back.”

    “I said listen to me!”

    All of a sudden, the sentry froze. Their weapon came down, and their spine racked straight again. All signs of a defensive posture were gone. Lena didn’t dwell on how odd it was for very long; She was done with this room, and more to the point, she was done with her situation. She simply couldn’t handle the thought of being a lab rat any longer. Surviving was her top priority, but in order to do that, she would have to decide.

    “Take me to the Keeper.” Lena demanded, “Tell him that his daughter is ready to talk.”

     

     

    The large -clunk- of the reinforced door brought Kara’s sleepy eyelids to attention.

    Smith backed her hips to the end of the cot in the corner of their cell, and with good reason, too. Uniforms had been coming in and out at least once a day to grab her by the arms and to drag her away for some unknown reason. Either she wouldn’t admit to what was going on upstairs because she was too afraid to, or because she felt guilty still. Kara knew that Smith was none too proud for the hand she had played in the capture of their kind, and thereby their progeny as well. It was a question Kara wrestled with for days on end— what else was Alex to do?

    Smith.

    Kara sat up, reminding herself as pairs of boots began to stomp their way in.

    Her name is Smith.

    Having learned by now to disable the biometric alarms while inside, the sentries felt emboldened to move closer to the bars separating them from their prisoners. They no longer had to shout for Smith to step forward and to come through the rolling door. Kara, on her part, had at least agreed to behave, lest they be gassed again. The pile of empty canisters at the back of their cell had grown. She hated even to look at them.

    Still, it was the not knowing that unsettled her down to her bones. Every time they brought Smith back, she was either too weak to stand or she wished to go straight to sleep.

    “Where are you taking her?” Kara asked, removing her hands from the bars just as soon as a sentry pointed a shock rod at her. “Hey,” She shook her head, “I just wanna know what’s going on. We’ve been in here for over a week—”

    “It’s none of your concern.” One of them replied as they fastened that godawful collar around Smith’s neck.

    “Like hell it isn’t!”

    “Quiet down.”

    “Where’s Lena?”

    The sentry bothering enough to speak to her spun around on the soles of his boots with a wide smirk at that, “What is your obsession with the Keeper’s daughter, anyway? You freak.”

    “I just wanna talk to her. Please—”

    “Yeah, well, she doesn’t want to talk to you.”

    Kara blinked, unbelieving of what she’d just heard, “W-what do you mean?”

    The sentry leaned closer, his brow pulling tightly together with a menacing sort of stare, “Ask yourself, old timer… Why is that you’re down here, and she’s up there?” His eyes shot skyward, while Kara’s heart dropped to her stomach. “Think about it. She left with just one of you and then came back with at least three more.”

    “No.” Kara shook her head.

    “She played you.”

    “No, I don’t believe you.”

    “I don’t care what you believe. She’s up there right now talking to the Keeper as we speak.”

    The rest of the sentries had filtered back out of the room while the one toying with Kara had, mistakenly, lagged behind. She reached out to grab at the edge of his flak jacket once he turned away to leave, and he came flying back to hit the bars hard with his back. Kara held him tightly there as he grunted with all of his effort to escape and hissed beside his ear, “You’re a liar.”

    It didn’t take long for the others to come to their comrade’s rescue, but it did take at least four of them to pry her arms away and to free him. They hit the biometric alarm on their way out for good measure, shouting incoherently at her that she’ll be dead soon, and other things of that nature. They left the alarm to sound for what felt like hours, until blood ran from Kara’s eardrums and she could neither see nor hear anything once it was disabled. Had it not been for her regenerative abilities, she would have been rendered blind and deaf in one fell swoop.

    She did end up learning something, though.

    The people here weren’t so keen on keeping her alive this time.

     

     

    “You’re looking well today.”

    “Mm.” Lionel scratched at the course, light brown hair of his freshly shaved goatee, “Yes.”

    He had been pacing in front of a holoscreen, studying various projections of some kind. Lena couldn’t make out the data from where she stood at the front of his office. At least they were alone now, and she didn’t have to contend with any more hardened personalities other than her father’s. His personal guard was less careful whenever escorting her around the compound, lately. It seemed their distaste for her ridiculing them in years past was something of a sore spot. Lena doubted very much that they would face much punishment at all for allowing her to “trip” or to “bump into walls” these days.

    “What is it you wish to say to me?” He asked.

    Lena knew the only reason she was allowed to stand there in the first place was because her father still clung to hope. However minuscule, it was the hope that she would one day see things his way. She knew that if that hope existed even in the slightest, then she had no choice but to latch onto it.

    “I actually wanted to ask you something.”

    Lionel huffed a laugh through his nose and finally deigned to turn around; both arms folded across his chest in a smug manner. “After everything you’ve put me through?”

    “I want to come back.”

    “Really, now. You truly believe that I would fall for your coming around so quickly?”

    “I’m still your daughter, am I not?”

    Lionel grunted and paced towards his desk. He took his cane up from where it was leaned there and eyed it with a steep sense of indignation. Never had she seen a more accomplished yet unsatisfied man. There would always be the need for “more” with him.

    “Am I all that’s left of your bloodline?” She dared to ask.

    Her father’s eyes instantly shot to her own, the hazel of his irises filling to the brim with resentment, “And look what you do with it,” He replied, “Such glory is wasted on you, I’m afraid.”

    “No,” Lena pleaded, “I was wrong to betray you. I see that now. All I wanted was to explore the outside world. I- I wanted to know what was out there- what I was missing all along.”

    “So, tell me, did you find what you were looking for?”

    Lena felt her hands shaking at her sides, so she clasped them tightly behind her back. “No…” Her eyes cast down towards her toes then as she hung her head, defeated, “Turns out it was here all along.”

    “What, exactly?”

    “…Greatness.”

    “Which is what you shall have.”

    Lena’s eyes went up from the floor. She felt her brow pinch together with disbelief, “You mean—”

    “Regaining the full measure of my health has opened my eyes.” Lionel gestured with a wide swing of his free hand back towards the holoscreen, “I cannot do this without you by my side. I need your intellect if we’re to dawn a new age upon mankind.”

    “Can I ask, where does J’o—” Lena cleared her throat suddenly, removing herself from the shape of their names altogether, “Where does the martian fit into all this?”

    “He’s the key, my dear.”

    “For—”

    “Shh, shh…” Lionel came to stand face-to-face with her and then tapped his cane atop her shoulder, ushering her down to her knees. Lena swallowed past a knot that had formed in her throat, but she did as she was instructed. “All in due time.” He replied, “I will say, however, that it was such a pleasure to finally lay eyes on the creature again after so many years. Synthesizing his genetic code was the bane of my existence for so long… It certainly aided my forgiving of you.”

    “Am- am I like him, now? Like… you? Are we even human anymore?”

    “You and I, dear daughter, are the true example of what my vaccine is capable of. Success after two centuries is still success; I don’t care to look at it any other way. Don’t you see? I’ve vaccinated the human condition. We’re gods.”

    Lena nodded, her reply breathy upon her lips, “I understand.”

    “Aren’t you going to thank me?” Lionel smiled down at her, gesturing the polished silver pommel of his cane towards her face. The very same cane that had killed her mother. Perhaps it was just her eyes playing tricks on her; It seemed to shine with the gloss of her mother’s blood.

    Terrified of any more of his maniacal explanations, Lena pressed forward to gently kiss her father’s cane. “Thank you.”

    “Still ‘daddy’, yes?”

    Lena pulled the corners of her mouth up into a smile. “Yes.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading <3

    Chapter 25: What the Heart Wants

    Chapter Notes

    TW: depictions of sudden death, violence, animal cruelty

    Chapter Twenty-Five: What the Heart Wants

     

    Kara could see for miles from up here.

    Or, kilometers, as Lena would so often remind her.

    “Two hundred years is no excuse not to keep up with the rest of the world, darling.”

    There went another harrumph through her nose. Darling, she thought… What a word. Kara wondered if Lena had truly meant any of it at all. She’d come across some of the most fantastical liars during her time— but none of them had been so skilled as to fool her completely.

    No, Lena would never.

    Kara adjusted her shoulder blades against the hard bars of her newest cage, refusing to give in to the hurt which sat cradled inside of her chest.

    She’s not like them.

    She loves me.

    A featherlight sprinkling of rain kissed the end of her nose, causing her to look up towards a dreary black sky. Swirls of grayish clouds roiled in the distance. The rain had been contemplating letting loose its downpour for some time now, but still, the fine mist persisted. Not so much as to dampen Kara’s clothes or even to darken them, but just enough to annoy her with the every-once-in-a-while swipe of her brow. She thought of her mother and father more frequently since being brought up to the surface, and higher yet. Up here, she felt closer to them.

    And should the lightning feel so obliged as to strike her, she might just rejoin them sooner than she planned.

    Lying beside her, Smith stirred with a groggy “ugh” as she wobbled upright to hold her head in her hands. One of her elbows went between the floor of bars beneath them and she shrieked with her surprise, righting herself again with a white-knuckled grip and a few quickly panted breaths. She looked down, bewildered.

    “Morning.” Kara grumbled.

    Smith blinked, “What?”

    The world below them spun slowly around, and then back the other way again. Kara had to give the Children of Liberty some credit. They were a creative bunch.

    Realization barreling like a freight train, Smith was stunned. At first, anyway. That sense of shock was swiftly slapped away. Irritation sat squarely upon her face instead.

    “This is your fault.” She accused.

    “Sure, blame it all on me.”

    “What’d you think they’d do, huh?! If you hadn’t kept destroying—”

    “Oh, shut up already! We wouldn’t even be here if you’d helped!”

    Smith growled beneath her breath and folded her arms across her chest. “Don’t start with that again.”

    “The two of us could’ve easily made it out.” Kara lowered her voice then, muttering to herself, “We’ve done it before. Plenty of times.”

    “I keep telling you…”

    Kara looked up.

    “I’m not your sister.”

    “Yeah.” Kara replied brokenly, “I’m starting to believe you.”

    The two of them sat in silence for a long time after that. Long enough for Kara’s head to nod forward several times with all of her tiredness. She’d not eaten in weeks. Muffled claps of faraway thunder lulled her to sleep here and there, but not fully enough so as to dream. The backs of her eyelids would strobe with pinks and purples from the throbbing flashes of lightning from up above, but still, the sky chose not to rain. Not yet.

    Smith sighed from her corner of their suspended cage, and Kara forced her eyes open again.

    “I’m starving.” Smith said eventually.

    Kara huffed a tired laugh and shifted to lay her back flat across the bars, “Lena and I made it all the way through two deserts before that nearly killed me, so I wouldn’t worry.”

    “If you say so. It’s just a little ironic, I guess. I built these damn things.” Smith fingered lightly at the expertly crafted joints of their metal cage, “I wonder how they’re gonna get us anything to eat all the way up here…”

    Kara turned her head to eye the long crane arm and the chain holding them aloft their lonely mountainside. The compound beneath them was a long way to fall, she noted inwardly. Quite the fortress, it was. Steep slopes on all but one side. A mountain all on its own, and they were at its very peak. Sentries and seekers alike moved in strict paths down below, much like ants in a colony. Kara stared at them, doing her best to make out each of their faces… and then she sat up.

    “Wait, what do you mean?”

    Smith shrugged, “I mean, unless they plan on using drones or something—”

    “Why would they do that?”

    “So we can eat? What’s wrong with you?”

    Kara shook her head, “Have you been eating?”

    “Yeah, every day. Haven’t you?”

    “…No.”

    “What?”

    “Is that where they’ve been taking you?” Kara asked dryly, “To feed you?”

    “I—” Smith faltered for a moment. There it was again, as plain as day. An ill-timed pang of guilt. “That, and they run tests. I don’t wanna talk about it.”

    “Listen, you don’t have to feel bad. They want me in a weakened state, that’s all.”

    “I just assumed…” Smith replied with a frown.

    “It’s fine. Not sure how they figured it out, but—”

    “Well, that part’s obvious, isn’t it?”

    Kara’s blood pressure skyrocketed at the insinuation. “No. I told you, she’s not like that.”

    “Okay. Cool. Let me know when you’re ready to take your stupid love goggles off so we can have a better chance of surviving this.”

    “Shut up.”

    “Hey, genius! Your girlfriend sold you out!”

    “Stop it!”

    A voice from below echoed upward then, cutting the tension in two. It was deep. Gravelly. Menacing.

    “I have to say, Smith, I am disappointed.”

    Smith’s head spun around at the sound and soon she was balanced on her knees, both hands clasped together as if in prayer. “Keeper, please! You have to believe me, I’m not involved—”

    His voice traveled the long slope of the mountain again, “Had you kept your condition to yourself, I would have been perfectly fine with keeping you in my employ.”

    “Sir, I’m begging you—”

    Kara rocked forward, unable to hold her tongue any longer. “Where’s Lena? What have you done with her?”

    She could almost see the pinch of anger beset between his bushy eyebrows. The Keeper grabbed at a passing sentry and kept him close. “My daughter is none of your concern. You will supply me with your genetic code, and I will, in turn, reward you with a quick and painless death.”

    Kara grit her teeth and called back down to him, “You’ll never get it.”

    “One drop of blood, remnant. That is all I need.”

    “Then you’ll have to wait a long time, you psychopath.”

    “Not as long as you might think.” There was the flash of his off-white teeth as he grinned, “I believe I may have found your one true weakness. So much has been revealed to me… I almost feel sorry for you.”

    “Liar!” Kara shouted, gripping the bars of the cage.

    “You will give me that stone skin of yours, or I will have your brethren tear you limb from limb- mark my words!”

    “Not if I get to you first!”

    Angered, the Keeper reached for the weapon in the sentry’s arms and then aimed it skyward, firing with a blind rage into the suspended cage. They weren’t the usual electrostatic rounds that Kara was so used to seeing during the Union’s struggle to hold the majority of the western railways, either. No, these were the simple kind. Barbaric. Ancient.

    Kara leapt across the cage to brace herself in front of Smith, shielding her from the gunfire. Only one bullet whizzed past and then ricocheted off the bars with a jarring -plink- sound. She shouted downwards from over her shoulder, “Hey! I’m the only one with ‘stone skin’ up here- you’ll kill her!”

    “Perhaps I should.” The Keeper replied, “Ungrateful abominations, the lot of you. Killing the world, and then each other! You were an accident!”

    What Kara mistook as a shrill howl of wind came sweeping around the edge of their cage all of a sudden, screeching mid-flight with a sharp, downward dive. A brown-feathered vengeance. Her dearest friend.

    “Peck—!”

    She could only watch as the raptor’s long talons lashed forward towards their prey. He was protective as a rule, the damned bird. There was no caution upon the full scope of his wingspan. He meant to crash into the Keeper with as much speed as the wind would seek to grant him.

    The flapping chaos of it all raged in the space of seconds. The Keeper’s arms swung time and time again, but Peck clawed and bit with every chance he could get. There was a terrible screaming sound, painful and agonizing, and then there was the loud clap of gunfire again. Kara braced herself around Smith again— and time nearly stood still.

    Kara looked over her shoulder once more, breathless. The Keeper’s weapon was on the ground, spent and smoking from its snubbed barrel. He stood there bent over in pain, clutching at his eyes, and finally, with one last angry swipe of his hands, he grabbed Peck clean from the air by his neck…

    …and wrung it all the way around.

    Breath made its way into Kara’s lungs at last, and with it, she wailed desolately in the place of words. Peck landed limply upon the hard ground with one wing splayed wide and the other folded beneath himself in an ungainly manner.

    “No, leave it!” She heard the Keeper order as the sentry went to clear the feathered mess at his feet. After that, there was the familiar echoey -pop- of a broken sound barrier, and then, the Keeper was gone. Soon, Peck’s lifeless body and Kara’s dreadful sobbing were all that was left.

    She cried for what felt like hours.

    Time, her greatest enemy, laughed in her face.

    The world had grown dark and starry again before Smith dared to say a word. She fumbled hand over hand across the bars and came to sit beside Kara as she wept. It only made sense— Kara had spoken many times about Peck and their adventures together. Smith seemed to not believe any of it at all. Until now.

    “I’m sorry, Kara.”

     

     

    So much fuss over something so small…

    Lena traced circles against the glass of her aquarium, watching the immortal jellyfish inside undulate to and fro. They propelled themselves in time with the rolling current of their tank, and Lena wondered as she stared at them… What would the world be like if no one had bothered to give them a second glance?

    Humans were never meant to live this long, she decided. It had been a mistake. A foolish attempt at maintaining power, and order. What might have begun as a desperate attempt to rid the world of a deadly virus had no doubt fallen into the wrong hands.

    Her father’s hands.

    It made her sick to know how old he really was, or rather, how long he had kept such a large and intimidating truth from her. Her siblings, her mother… How much of this man’s influence had scarred the earth, as well as the people that were left to suffer upon it? Perhaps there was a reason she couldn’t remember enough of her childhood. What kinds of horrors had her subconscious tucked away inside little boxes, never to be seen again?

    Lena sighed despondently and rested her chin upon her folded arms again. She stared longways across her bedroom with a forlorn look, happy to see it again but also sad to have returned. Her every other thought was of Kara. And Sara. And Barry. She cried for them— but not for Nyssa, the backstabbing bitch. Teleporting for the hills at the first sign of capture was just like her. Lena felt a rage bubble up the length of her throat every time the memory of those ephemeral portals cropped up.

    The pristine guard of her display sabre stared back at her, and she wished to pluck it from the wall and all but charge outside, but the single sentry stationed outside her room would surely stop her in her tracks. That is, if the buzzing feeling from inside of her chest every time she felt anger didn’t stop her first. Lena had given little thought towards the after-effects of the ghastly serum she had been injected with. Another of her father’s failures, she supposed. No doubt having decimated her immune system much like his own. But he was well again, and so she assumed that she would be too, given time. What she wouldn’t give for her throat to stop hurting in the meantime, though… but at least the nausea was gone.

    -bang- -bang-

    Lena sat up straight at the sound of a fist against her large ornate door and resolved to see who it was. She was free to work for her father and to move about her own room again, yes, but that was about as far as her freedom extended. Sunlight hadn’t graced the side of her face in at least a week. Maybe two. Not with the brewing storm, anyway. The Keeper apparently trusted that she’d be no more of a nuisance than the rest of his chattel, though, Lena very much doubted that he’d believed her to be absolutely no threat at all. Still, no one had come to take the decorative sabre from her room. She wondered how sharp it was, if at all.

    The door came open to the same sour expression, day in and day out. The sentry’s name was Knox. He was a tight-lipped bearded fellow who possessed zero qualms with shoving her around from place to place. Lena had grown tired of that part in particular.

    “Science Lab A.” He barked.

    Lena blinked, “I’m supposed to be handling this week’s intake—”

    “New arrivals from last night, no intake.” His brow pulled together tightly, “Science. Lab. A.”

    “How do you expect me to run those kinds of tests if we haven’t assessed their risk level yet?”

    Knox impatiently reached out for her, “Get moving.”

    “Don’t touch me—” Lena dodged him, “I can walk on my own.”

    “I don’t care. Move.”

    “Stop—” Knox came at her with both hands that time, moving from the doorway and inside the room. Lena decided to shout. “Stop!”

    As if slapped across the face, Knox’s hardened expression was all but swept clean. He backed away from Lena in an instant and stilled his hands at his sides.

    On the inside, Lena was still raging. “You will not touch me again.” She seethed at him. The burning in her throat, oddly enough, felt suddenly soothed. Every word was like a gentle balm.

    Knox shook his head in awkward agreement and then mumbled the word, “No.”

    “Do you hear me?”

    He nodded with the same blank stare, “Yes.”

    Lena paused for a moment. “Knox?”

    “Yes ma’am…”

    “What’s the matter?”

    “Nothing, ma’am.”

    Her curiosity spiked with his sudden mood to acquiesce her every demand. It was as if she were controlling him. Impossible… Lena thought.

    “Close the door.”

    He turned immediately and did exactly as he was told.

    “Now, open it.”

    Again, he obeyed. Shockingly, there wasn’t a single ounce of confusion apparent in his eyes. It was simple obedience, Lena realized. The detached and emotionless kind… and all she had to do was speak to him. The path ahead was clear now. She knew exactly what to do.

    “Knox, I want you to listen very carefully to me.”

    “Yes…”

    “You’re going to take me to the eastern gate, but should we be stopped by anyone else on our way, I want you to tell them that you’re taking me to my fencing lesson.”

    “Understood, ma’am.”

    “Do not alert the other sentries.” Her voice felt warm on its way out. A tender thrumming upon every syllable. It felt good.

    “No, ma’am.”

    “Wait there.”

    Quickly, Lena made leaps and bounds around the furniture of her room towards the large arch of her hearth. There above it sat her display sabre, affixed to a carved wooden plaque. A layer of dust slipped from the curve of his hilt guard, and she blew there for good measure. Her thumb went to the blade. It was blunt. She glowered at the feeling of it. Still, it would provide her more protection than her bare hands would, however skilled they were. She wasted no time in making for the door again and took only a few seconds longer to pull more of her blouse out from the tight confines of her waist. Her long sleeves billowed like an old ship’s sail, a light canvas contrast to the dark color of her pants. Lena shoved her sabre against Knox’s chest, willing him to hold it. He caught it clumsily as she yanked the door open again and then stepped through without looking back.

    It didn’t take long for her to stop, having noticed the lack of footsteps behind her own. There was, however, the sudden “caw” of two large blackbirds landing just outside the foggy panes of her bedroom window. Strange, she thought… I wonder what they want all the way up here.

    She backpedaled quickly and waved an arm towards Knox, hurrying him along, “Well? Don’t just stand there!”

    “You said to wait, ma’am.” He blinked slowly with his reply, still as humbled and emotionless as the day was long.

    Lena gave a sharp -tsk- from the backs of her teeth and then reeled forward to take him by the elbow for once. “Come on!”

     

     

    It had finally begun to rain.

    Kara found the only times that she was willing to speak, or to make any noise at all, was whenever she was screaming at the armed uniforms passing down below. They kicked Peck’s body to the side every so often with their muddy boots just to torture her, having been ordered to leave it exactly where it was. An awful reminder for Kara; Defy the keeper and face a fate worse than death.  

    There was a vibration along the bars where she sat, rumbling an unsettling feeling deep into her hip bones. She thought maybe it was the rolling thunder coming closer, but then she looked over towards Smith who was huddled into a fetal position. She was clinging to her arms and legs, shivering violently. The rain had no doubt chilled her to the bone. Kara remembered then that her sister didn’t have the same ability to regulate her own body temperature. That, unfortunately, seemed specific to Kara’s “condition”. The more Smith shivered, the more the bars beneath her vibrated. She and the metal had always been one, but this? This was painful to watch.

    “I’m like a space heater over here, you know.” Kara said, “I won’t mind.”

    Without a word, Smith unfurled herself and scrambled across the bars to press herself fully into Kara’s side. Her fingers and lips were a light shade of blue, and Kara went as far as to clasp Smith’s hands between her own to warm them up. It wasn’t long before the shivering stopped. There was only the odd, pained whimper any time Smith tried moving. The days they’d spent suspended and swinging over the compound in their metal cage had bruised Smith from head to toe. There was no comfortable angle at which to sleep. Kara’s pain receptors, like her internal body temperature, were controlled with a matter of ease. It would still be weeks yet before starvation would begin wreaking havoc on her abilities.

    “Thank you.”

    “I can’t stop thinking about what they’re gonna try to do just to get my blood.” Kara responded with a sullen tone.

    “I dunno, but don’t give it to them if you can help it.”

    “What? Why the change of heart?”

    “I just don’t think the world can handle two of you, after all. That’s all. Let alone an entire army.”

    Kara threw her head back with a laugh. “Yeah, imagine all of the angry and insecure tanks running around.”

    Soon, the smiles fell from both of their faces. It wasn’t a very funny thought, actually. In fact, it held some truth to it. Should the Keeper be able to extract what he wanted from Kara’s blood, he would no doubt use it for himself, and then for his horde. Kara frowned. She missed Lena. Nothing in two hundred years had made her heart ache so much besides the loss of her sister.

    “I miss her.” Kara said, nearly a whisper. She waited for Smith to berate her for her foolishness somehow, for the sad lump that she was, heartbroken and longing for someone who —for a lack of knowing otherwise— didn’t appear to want her back. But instead, Smith simply asked her a question.

    “Did you love her?”

    Kara clenched her jaw to reroute the pain from behind her eyes. She wanted to cry, but was doing everything in her power not to. “More than anything.” She replied.

    “It’s funny,” Smith tried sitting up finally, “I’ve always had this weird feeling that, I dunno, like my body’s here, but for some reason, my heart’s somewhere else. Like it… Oh, forget it.” She trailed off.

    “Like it belongs with someone else?” Kara finished.

    “Mm- yeah. I guess so.”

    “That’s because it did. For a time, anyway.”

    Smith didn’t seem to be so offput about discussing her past this time. She didn’t shirk away from Kara, or scoff, or roll her eyes either. It seemed like she had a question that had gone unanswered for a long time, and that, apparently, was enough for her to consider hearing Kara out.

    “Alright. Assuming that I believe everything you’ve told me so far… Where- I mean ‘who’ might have it?”

    “What, your heart?”

    There came the sound of that dependable scoff. Smith turned to leave only her shoulder laying against Kara’s. “Never mind.”

    “No, wait- I’m not making fun of you. God, you really are so much like her, even now.”

    “Your sister?”

    “Yeah.”

    “Whatever…” Smith replied, “Would you just tell me?”

    “Okay… Her name was Maggie. She was,” Kara cleared her throat carefully, “…human.”

    Smith hung her head slightly. “Oh. So, she’s dead, I’m guessing?”

    “No.”

    “I don’t understand. Was it because I ended up here?”

    “No, it was before that.” When Smith turned her head to look Kara in the eyes, there wasn’t even a faint semblance of annoyance in her own. Her black eyes often gave her away. She couldn’t hide how she felt. Not from Kara. “Well,” Kara explained, “She left.”

    “Ah.”

    “She loved you, don’t get me wrong, but for her… it wasn’t enough.”

    “What’s that supposed to mean?”

    “Back then it was hard for you two. The world was still so divided, and even though we weren’t as underground as we are today, Maggie faced a lot of danger just for being with you.”

    “I couldn’t protect her?”

    “She didn’t want you to always have to. That was the whole point.”

    “…Oh.”

    “They did more than just brand sympathizers in those days, remember?”

    “That I remember, yeah.”

    “How do you still feel that kind of heartache after a hundred and fifty years?”

    “I…” Smith laid her head back again, “I dunno. Kinda sucks, to be honest.”

    “I can imagine.”

    “I mean, you won’t have to in another fifty years or so.”

    “Hm?” Kara hummed as she pulled some of her sopping golden hair from her eyes. Something flew by the cage again, but the wind was nearly stagnant for all of the continuing rainfall. Ravens, Kara realized. They seemed to be flocking towards the tall battlements surrounding the compound.

    “The Keeper’s daughter? Kinda icky that I watched her grow up and everything—”

    “Yeah, don’t remind me.” Kara interrupted, following the greasy tail feathers which continued to pass them by.

    “All I’m saying is- this feeling? It’s like someone’s torn a hole right through the middle of me. I never knew what it was, but now that I do, I wouldn’t wish it on anyone. You know she’ll die, right? Eventually?”

    Kara sighed. “Yeah. I know.”

    “Suit yourself, then.” Smith craned her head to look through the curtains of falling rain and then puzzled her expression as she asked, “Holy shit. What’s with all the birds?”

    Kara sighed again. “You’ll see.”

    Chapter End Notes

    A lil weepy editing this one. Don't worry, there's more to come.

    Thanks for reading. <3

    Chapter 26: What We’re Made Of

    Chapter Notes

    TW: violence, blood, graphic depictions of death

    Chapter Twenty-Six: What We’re Made Of

     

    They walked beneath the covered walkways towards the east gate.

    Rain fell in silver sheets all around them, crashing against the sloped rock and streaming down towards the foot of their small mountain. Thunder rolled above as water rushed past their boots in restless streams. Lena felt the steady pounding of her heart against her ribs, until its rhythm seemed to merge with the storm itself. Wind, rain, and thunder swallowed every other sound. The whole world trembled around her, and somehow, in the middle of it all, she felt welcomed by it. No longer afraid.

    Knox kept a severe face as they passed one guard station after another. He meant to do exactly as Lena had instructed, unaware that her true destination was now the forge. It was the only place she could think of to find Smith— assuming her father hadn’t killed her. Or worse.

    He wouldn’t have thrown her down below, would he? Their prized Sentinel had been in his service for as long as Lena could remember, always soot-covered from the forge and ready for battle.

    Lena forced the thought away before her fear could take hold. If Smith wasn’t there, then she would go down to the sublevels.

    She had to get to them.

    Soon they reached the forge’s angled eaves. Lena peered inside and saw no light at all. The sight of it struck her with the same feeling the last two times she had found herself there that year— with a dull yet gut-wrenching disappointment. There was nobody inside except for the glint of her road-tested sabre, shining like a beacon through the deluge. Her first instinct was to run for it and trade it for the decorative blade tucked behind Knox’s belt, but before she could even turn to point it out, the rain —so violent it had been splashing upward from the ground— suddenly stopped.

    For a heartbeat, there was only silence.

    Then the shadow passed overhead.

    A mass of black feathers swept above them, blotting out what little light remained and plunging them into darkness. After that, the ground began to shake.

    At first it was faint, but it grew stronger with every second. Lena wondered if a tornado was carving a devastating path across the mountainside, but then the guttural yowls and cries of predator cats cut through the wind, erasing the thought from her mind altogether. She pressed closer to Knox and urged him onward, keeping tight to his side as the danger closed in.

    “Take me to the gate. Stop anyone in uniform who gets in our way.”

    “Yes ma’am.”

    By the time they reached it, the echoes of gunfire against screams were all but gone. There was something heavy pushing against the large, barricaded track, and Lena shielded her eyes from the rain to look straight up towards the towering battlement. She saw someone there and reacted immediately.

    “Tell them to open it.”

    Knox fired a warning shot beside the last sentry clutching a wide parapet with both arms. “Open the gate!” He shouted, “Now!”

    “No!” The sentry screamed back. His cap had gone from his head, and his thick layer of curls were now flattened with all of the rain, falling clumsily into his eyes. “Y-you’ll let them in!”

    “I gave you an order!”

    “But they’ll kill us all!”

    -pop-

    Knox fired again without skipping a beat. The round went cleanly through the sentry’s forehead, dropping him from the parapet like a lost sack of grain. He fell over the battlement and all the way down to land with a -splash- into the mud, causing Lena to shriek and cover her eyes.

    No… please, no.

    Forgive me.

    How many would continue to die at her hand? Inadvertently or not, human or meta, every life mattered. She wished that she could take it back. To rearrange her words, or to pacify Knox’s inherent violence completely. But how?

    “Keep behind me, ma’am.” Knox said, dragging her along towards the staircase. It seemed that nothing would stop him from completing his mission.

    They made haste climbing the rusted steps. Lena struggled to keep pace with Knox, who proved far more battle-tested than she had first assumed. He moved across the scaffolding with practiced ease, not so much as making a single misstep. It was all she could do just to cling to every stretch of railing she could reach. Rain slicked the metal, and Lena feared she might slip at any moment. She could hear more of their enemy reaching the gate and was quick to turn back through the door to see how many— but a familiar, whiskered snarl met her there on the rampart.

    A low growl rolled from the soaked cougar as it pressed towards the door, blood-red froth sliding from its long fangs. Lena had only a moment to feel Knox step behind her and raise the muzzle of his gun.

    “No!” Her hand closed on the gun as Knox fired, the recoil of which jolting through the bones of her fingers.

    The pain sparked a fury inside of her, but still, she stood between the hunter and hunted, refusing to let either of them do each other any harm. The cat certainly meant her no ill will, as its eyes were trained only upon Knox.

    “Ma’am! Are you alright?”

    Lena reached for the strap of Knox’s gun and abruptly yanked it from his shoulder, “Give me that!”

    Over the battlement it went, hurled as far onto the other side and into the trees as possible. Lena was eager to be rid of the damn thing, and any others that she could find. Barbaric machines, guns. Obedient Knox went to stand at the command station then, ready to draw open the gate upon Lena’s command, and Lena placed her uninjured hand at the door, ready to shut him in.

    “I want you to stay here where it’s safe.” She said.

    Knox spun around with a hurt expression towards her, “But ma’am—”

    “Do as I say.”

    “Miss Luthor!”

    Lena stopped, gobsmacked.

    “Lena…” He bowed.

    She was astonished by Knox’s newfound sense of urgency. It was as if emotion had crept back into his subconscious, making him small, and less menacing. “What is it?” She asked.

    “Take me with you.”

    “No. I need you to stay where—”

    “I love you.”

    Lena fumbled over the end of her sentence, appalled by Knox yet again. He dropped to his knees and began to form tears in his eyes. Something was very wrong. Had Lena done something to him which couldn’t be fixed? She felt responsible, of course. Had she not gentled this man so much, then he might still have been able to defend himself should he need to.

    “Promise me something?”

    “Anything, ma’am.”

    “Promise me that you’ll stay here. No matter what.”

    “But—”

    “Promise me, Knox.”

    Knox hung his head, his fat bottom lip sticking out with a disheartened pout, “I promise.”

    “Now, open the gate.”

    Without so much as looking over his shoulder, teary-eyed Knox reached for the toggle switch to bring the gate open. It shook the base of the tower as the chain and pulley system engaged, and then the sounds of screaming erupted again. Lena slammed the door behind herself, shutting the echoes of Knox’s sobs inside, and ran down the staircase with the cougar in tow. They seemed to be guiding her, running just up ahead beneath the rampart wall so that they might encounter any resistance first. All of a sudden, the gate creaked, and then groaned, and then broke away from the track entirely.

    It was what poured inside that made Lena nearly stumble forward.

    Sentries on the ground turned tail and rain in the opposite direction… and with good reason, too.

    Even on all fours, a grizzly bear was still three times the size of a human man. One may have been a reasonable fight in some regard, but half a dozen barreling over the bent and twisted gate, Lena supposed, was the stuff of nightmares. Rumbling hoofbeats followed right behind, where the bears were all but overshadowed by a herd of running elk. They stampeded their way inside, intent on goring anyone in their way with their large racks of spiny antlers. Lena’s companion caught up with the following wave of cougars, who climbed the nearby scaffolding in search of sentry-shaped dinner. Last to enter were the scurrying flocks of chipmunks, squirrels, and ferrets, happy just to join the stormy chaos with their own brand of mischief.

    Lena slid to an awkward halt in front of the broken gate and squinted through the rain. It was falling so hard that it seemed to draw the color of the mud up from the ground, casting it into the surrounding air. All she could see was gray.

    “Ruby!” She called out into the storm, “Ruby- where are you?!”

    Arms suddenly went around her waist, the sound of a small “oof” shortly behind. Lena looked down to witness buoyant, rosy cheeks, dark hair, and a smile like the sun. Lena huffed a relief-filled breath, “I knew it was you. I- I can’t believe you’re here.”

    Ruby hugged her again, “About time you let us in.”

    “How,” Lena stammered, “How did you- but, I thought—”

    “Nyssa came to find us.” Ruby looked up with a sheepish grin, “Sorry it took so long.”

    Lena hugged her as tightly as she could and then wiped the rain from her eyes, “No, it’s alright- I’m just glad to see you.”

    “I’ve never called for so much help before, so I wasn’t sure who’d show up.”

    “Well, I think they heard you loud and clear.” Lena laughed as a chipmunk scurried up the length of her pant leg. “We need to get going—” Then she stopped just as she caught sight of Ava running towards them. One of Nyssa’s portals drew open just out of arm’s reach, snaking black tendrils around the falling rain.

    Both of them arrived at the same time, with the same question quick to fall from their lips, “Where’s Sara?”

     

     

    It was strange to hear rainfall midflight.

    The silent lashing of it as it fell towards the earth, passing through the bars of the cage… The occasional -boom- of thunder rolled across the mountains, a reminder that the storm was alive and well. The eerie screams from below made Kara pause— and she realized she’d been right about the ravens. The cavalry had arrived.

    “This is crazy…” Smith drawled as she looked towards the ground. Groups of elk were chasing people into buildings. “What the hell’s happening?”

    “That’s our cue.” Kara replied.

    “What?”

    “We need to get down there and help.”

    “Hah!” Smith hacked a laugh, “Sure, I could get us out, but not from all the way up here!”

    “Then take us down.”

    Smith deadpanned in silence for a long moment, blinking slowly, “Yep. You’ve lost it.”

    “We don’t have a choice.”

    “You said it yourself- you’re the only person who’s one hundred percent unbreakable!”

    Kara shook her head. “I’ll keep you safe.”

    “How can you be so sure?”

    “Because… I won’t lose you a second time.”

    The universe could indeed be cruel, Kara thought. Flashes of crumbling concrete and broken pylons played like a horror movie inside of her head. The towering structure of a parking garage, falling floor by floor all the way down to crush them both…

    “I don’t know—” Smith said.

    “Trust me,” Kara held her arms out wide, urging her to come close, “…please.”

    Smith gave a reluctant nod and then pressed her shoulders in. “So, what, you’re gonna break my fall?”

    “Mhm.”

    “You’d do that for me? After I…” Smith shrugged then, trailing off into another guilt-laden silence.

    “Of course.” Kara replied. “I’d do anything for you. You’re my sister.”

    “…Okay, then.”

    “Ready?”

    “Nope.”

    “Do it anyway.” Kara gave her a squeeze, “I’ve got you.”

    With one long, deep breath, Smith craned her head to look up towards the chain holding their lonely cage aloft, and with her thumb and forefinger pinched together, she snapped them apart in the blink of an eye… which snapped the bulky chain completely in half.

    The sound of the wind falling past Kara’s ears was jarring enough, but that wasn’t as bad as trying to draw in breath while falling at terminal velocity. Kara had just enough time to cradle Smith’s neck steady as she landed with both feet on the ground, ducking just low enough for the top of the cage to bow inwards upon impact. Smith all but rolled out of Kara’s arms and scrambled to stand up straight.

    “Right.” She said a bit shakily. Her fists came up to face each other then and she pulled them apart, prying open a hole in the cage as easily as one would tear into a bread bag.

    It was unfortunate that they each had only seconds to collect themselves before the fighting began.

    Smith seemed to be fond of extracting the magazines from every sentry’s gun that she could put eyes on, flinging them far across the compound for good measure. What rounds that were spent in the aftermath she brought to a screeching halt midair, and those that were still chambered found themselves to be irrevocably jammed. Kara was amazed. It had taken her sister nearly a century of fumbling around her gift to be able to manipulate metals with such ease. Alex, or Smith, had become so attuned to iron (both in its natural and alloyed forms) that she had once said that she could taste it. It was good to see that she hadn’t lost her stride in that respect.

    Kara did what she could not to cause any fatalities, especially since the rain had finally begun to draw back in its intensity and she could see more clearly now where she was throwing— but they just kept coming. One after another beat her with batons, fists, and boots. Kara broke through them like the armored vessel that she was, similar in size, but devastatingly overpowered. She loved being underestimated.

    The endless combat became that of a blur the longer that it went on, until the dozens were whittled down to a handful, and that handful eventually resulted with one last adversary standing between them and the way ahead. Smith fell backwards at the sight of a grizzly bear charging past, and Kara was just about to shout for her to stand again when the last sentry raised his shock rod to strike her down. Kara lunged forward to pummel him in the chest, but not before the piercing end of a sword ran through the brunt of his shoulder. The sentry screamed and then rocked his head forward with a blow from his crown, and then he fell flat onto his face.

    Lena stood there in his place.

    A bolt of lightning illuminated her with a heavenly glow, and she panted tiredly with her sword arm still outstretched. Her thousand-yard-stare remained unbroken. Kara held her hands up in surrender, noticing all too late how the end of Lena’s blade had parked itself right inside the hollow notch between her collar bones. The silver glint of her sister’s insignia called out from the sabre’s hilt like a warning. She let the moment pass, too afraid to take another step.

    “I see you got your sword back.” Kara said with her chest still heaving.

    With the storm drawing farther away, inevitably throwing more time and space between the flash of lighting and crack of thunder, Lena stilled long enough to return to her sense of better judgement. There was that look in her eye again, and Kara begged for it to be true.

    Come back to me.

    Breathless, Lena’s sword fell from her hands to land with a sharp -clang- against the ground, and then she threw herself body and spirit into Kara, kissing her with a whimpered sense of relief.

    “Lena…” Kara stumbled back a step and breathed in the woman that she loved. Weeks had gone by with the thought of never seeing her again, but there she was. A force of nature, all on her own. “Oh, my God,” She kissed across Lena’s cheek to press her face there into her neck, “I knew you hadn’t turned your back on us.”

    “No, I swear.” Lena wept quietly against Kara’s ear, “I love you.” She kissed her over and over, “I love you, I love you—”

    “Sorry to break up the reunion…” Smith’s voice came from behind them, “But we should probably get moving.”

    Lena tugged at Kara’s arm then, begging her to listen, “Kara, my father’s more dangerous than we thought- he’s the one who created the vaccine two hundred years ago. He’s been extracting metahuman DNA somehow to sustain his own life, but now that he has J’onn’s genetic material- he’s been stealing their powers. He thinks he’s a god. We have to stop him.”

    Kara froze.

    Sara.

    It dawned on her, finally, Lionel’s grandiose claims of knowing Kara’s “one true weakness”… If he’d stolen Sara’s ability to siphon memories, then that only meant that he knew about everything.

    And everyone.

    She kissed Lena at the hollow of her cheek one last time. “Let’s get the others and get out of here, fast.”

    “Ava and Nyssa have Ruby- they’re waiting for us near the testing annex. Sara’s got to be in one of the observation dorms just inside. Barry, too.”

    Alex limped her way forward, “No time like the present, I guess.”

     

     

    Until now, Lena had never fully considered the extent of Ruby’s abilities— nor that they might have limits.

    A bull elk had seen fit to place himself right in their path, likely confused by all of the chaos and upset as well for all of the carnage. Kara, like always, was protective to a fault. She had stepped in front of the charging beast and grappled with his immense rack of antlers to keep him at bay. A battle of strength ensued, with Kara’s opponent being at least twice her size. Smith had struggled to move out of the way as quickly as possible, but without her brace, she was sluggish at best. Lena felt that it was odd to be in such close quarters with her as they dodged the light sprint of a cougar, or heavy lope of a bear… Someone had even fallen from a great height and landed nearly on top of her, but still Smith dragged herself back to her feet and reached for Lena’s hand, time and time again, putting herself directly in harm’s way. It was hard to imagine that this was the same blacksmith who wouldn’t so much as look at Lena, even on a good day. Regardless, Kara and her sister were intent on keeping her safe. Bravery was the stuff they were made of.

    “Kara, through here!” Smith shouted from beneath a rolling bay door. She was pulling it up from the ground with the available chain in hand, and Lena ran fast to assist her.

    Kara grunted with her effort to push the bull back, “M’kay! Ungh—” She pushed again, nearly butting heads with the creature, “J-just a second!”

    “Forget it, come on!”

    “Alright already!"

    With one last hard shove, Kara managed to put some distance between herself and the bull to turn and run for the door… but not enough, it would seem. For such a huge animal, he was incredibly quick to recover. Lena could nearly see her own reflection in his giant, black marbles for eyes by the time the door came back down between them. It caved in violently a mere second later with all of the bull’s forward momentum, sending one sharp, bony spine of his rack piercing right through it.
